Picturing general quantum subsystems
Octave Mestoudjian, Matt Wilson, Augustin Vanrietvelde, Pablo Arrighi

Abstract
We extend the usual process-theoretic view on locality and causality in subsystems (based on the tensor product case) to general quantum systems (i.e. possibly non-factor, finite-dimensional Von Neumann algebras). To do so, we introduce a primitive notion of splitting maps within dagger symmetric monoidal categories. Splitting maps give rise to subsystems that admit comparison via a preorder called comprehension, and support an adaptation of the usual categorical trace. We show that the comprehension preorder precisely captures the inclusion partial order between Von Neumann algebras, and that the splitting map trace captures the natural notion of Von Neumann algebra trace. As a consequence of the development of these diagrammatic tools, we prove that the known equivalence between semi-causality and semi-localisability for factor subsystems extends to all (including non-factor) subsystems.



1
Introduction

The notions of system and the rules by which they can be composed or decomposed are at the foundation of any physical theory. In quantum theory these rules challenge our preconceived notions of subsystems: a system
AB
, for instance, cannot be understood as just the sum of its parts
A
and
B
in the classical sense, but also describes quantum correlations between them. Traditionally, the composition of quantum systems is represented in terms of the tensor product of Hilbert spaces, whereas the decomposition is represented in terms of subalgebras of the algebra of operators on the Hilbert space at hand.

When considering that these algebras should be factor, the two approaches coincide and are represented using the quantum circuit model
[
22
]
, which is standardly used in quantum computation
[
37
]
, quantum information theory
[
51
]
and quantum foundations
[
16
,
29
]
. From the physical perspective, factor algebras and their associated circuit representation equip quantum theory with the fundamental notions of subsystem, state-reduction, locality, and causality, used in quantum foundations topics such as quantum reference frames
[
10
,
28
,
2
,
13
]
, reconstruction programs
[
16
,
42
]
, indefinite causal structures
[
15
,
38
]
, quantum cellular automata
[
27
,
40
,
6
]
, etc.

However, many situations unravel the limiting aspects of this standard definition of subsystem, even in finite dimensional quantum theory. Some quantum evolutions have a causal structure that can only be made visible when mixing together the tensor product and direct sum
[
34
,
48
,
8
]
. Many physical state spaces satisfy some superselection rules that are not respected by the tensor product composition, the most common example being the parity conservation for fermions
[
23
,
25
]
. In quantum gravity, where superpositions of geometries (of space-time) are often described as superposition of graphs, the neighbours of a node exist in superposition, and so cannot be factored into naive tensor products
[
5
,
12
]
.

A more general definition of subsystem in quantum theory, and one that solves the aforementioned issues, is to consider that a subalgebra representing a subsystem does not have to be a factor. The treatment of general quantum subsystems such as these in terms of compositional or diagrammatic principles has received some attention, for instance via representation of algebras as monoids with properties and the associated CP* construction
[
49
,
19
,
21
]
, the development of routed quantum circuits
[
45
,
48
]
and its categorical generalisation
[
52
]
, the use of graphical languages for
2
-categories
[
18
,
3
,
39
]
, and in the many-worlds calculus
[
14
]
. The understanding of general subsystems is however less well developed than it is for the factor case: in particular, somewhat suprisingly, there is no pictorial representation in the literature of the basic notion of splitting a system into parts, and of the natural pictorial notions of locality and causality that ought to come with such a representation. The formalisation of such a concept would likely give a natural route to extending the reasoning tools for tensor products in categorical quantum mechanics
[
20
,
31
,
1
]
to the non-factor setting, and further a natural route to the generalisation of non-factor subsystems to theories beyond quantum, such as those that fit within the frameworks of generalised
[
9
]
, operational
[
16
]
, or categorical probabilistic theories
[
29
]
. A satisfactory treatment of the notion, would likely contribute to the attempt to gain a clearer understanding of what exactly it means, formally, theory-independently, and compositionally, to be a subsystem
[
33
,
17
,
30
]
.

In this article, we find a simple set of diagrammatic tools for picturing and reasoning about locality and causality in general non-factor systems. To do so, we leverage the representation of factors in the circuit model, by defining formal internalised splitting maps which consist of embeddings of a base system into a bigger system that is equipped with a standard factor decomposition. These splitting maps allow us to derive, through diagrammatic definitions and from the factorisation of the bigger space, non-factor decompositions of the initial space; and to develop associated notions of locality and inclusion between subsystems. In order to demonstrate that this purely pictorial theory of subsystems recovers the algebraic approach when applied to quantum theory, we establish an equivalence between subsystems as diagrammatic splitting maps in quantum theory and subsystems as Von Neumann subalgebras, as they are more usually conceptualised
[
50
,
55
,
54
,
17
,
47
]
.

We show how these tools can be used to prove basic structural theorems about locality and causality. A fundamental result on the nature of causality in quantum theory is the equivalence, for a bipartite quantum channel between factor subsystems, of non-signalling from one system to the other and semi-localisability
[
24
,
11
,
41
]
, which demonstrates that information-theoretic non-signalling can be framed purely at the compositional level. We use the diagrammatic tools that we developed to prove that this equivalence between semi-causality and semi-localisability lifts from factor systems to arbitrary Von Neumann algebras, proving as a result the equivalence for general quantum systems which naturally arise for instance in superpositions of geometries.

The plan of the paper is as follows. In the second section, we explain how subsystems can naturally be seen as Von Neumann algebras and provide a few results on the structure of such subsystems. In the third section, we introduce the formalism of splitting maps that allows us to talk about diagrammatic subsystems. In the fourth section we show that the formalism of splitting maps is strictly equivalent to the one of subsystems as Von Neumann algebras. And in the fifth section, we define the notion of trace of a splitting map, show that it is equivalent to the usual trace over a Von Neumann algebra and use the formalism of splitting maps to provide a causal decomposition for maps that are non-signalling between general subsystems.

2
General Quantum Subsystems as Von Neumann algebras

All the Hilbert spaces and algebras considered in this article are finite-dimensional. We will now remind a few basic facts on the structure of these algebras
[
26
]
that represent quantum systems.

Definition 2.1

(* algebra)
.

A finite dimensional * algebra
\mathcal{A}
is a finite-dimensional complex algebra equipped with an involution
\dagger
such that:

•

for all
a,b
in
\mathcal{A}
,
(a+b)^{\dagger}=a^{\dagger}+b^{\dagger}

•

for all
a,b
in
\mathcal{A}
,
(ab)^{\dagger}=b^{\dagger}a^{\dagger}

•

for all
a
in
\mathcal{A}
and
\lambda
in
\mathbb{C}
,
(\lambda a)^{\dagger}=\overline{\lambda}a^{\dagger}

A * subalgebra
\mathcal{B}
of
\mathcal{A}
is a subalgebra of
\mathcal{A}
equipped with the involution
\dagger
of
\mathcal{A}
and closed under it. This is the notion of subalgebra that we will use in the rest of this article and we will write it
\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
. Moreover a subalgebra
\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
is said to be unital if
\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{B}}=\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{A}}
, i.e.
\mathcal{B}
has the same unit element as
\mathcal{A}
.

Definition 2.2

(Von Neumann Algebra)
.

A finite dimensional Von Neumann algebra is a unital * subalgebra of some
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
with, as involution, the usual adjoint coming from the inner product on
\mathcal{H}
. Let us call
\vnalg(\mathcal{H})
the set of Von Neumann algebras
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
.

Note that in finite dimension, a Von Neumann algebra is the same thing as a C* algebra. Let us give two examples of Von Neumann algebras acting on
\mathcal{H}
, coming from a decomposition of the space either as a tensor product or as a direct sum. Suppose that
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}_{\LL}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\R}
, then one can define the Von Neumann algebra of operators acting on the left part of the tensor
\mathcal{A}_{\otimes}=\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\LL})\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\R}}
. Similarly suppose that
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{2}
, then one can define the Von Neumann algebra
\mathcal{A}_{\oplus}=\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{1})\oplus\mathbb{C}\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{2}}
of operators that leave invariant
\mathcal{H}_{1}
and
\mathcal{H}_{2}
and moreover can only act as homotheties on
\mathcal{H}_{2}
.

Definition 2.3

(Commutant and centre)
.

Let
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
be a Von Neumann algebra. The commutant of
\mathcal{A}
(within
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
) is the algebra
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}:=\{b\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})|\forall a\in\mathcal{A},ab=ba\}
of operators on
\mathcal{H}
that commute with every element of
\mathcal{A}
. The centre of
\mathcal{A}
is the commutative algebra
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{A}):=\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
of the elements of
\mathcal{A}
that commute with all elements of
\mathcal{A}
. Remark that due to Von Neumann’s bicommutant theorem
[
44
]
,
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{A})=\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{A}^{\prime}=\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})
. We say that
\mathcal{A}
is factor when
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{A})=\mathbb{C}\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}}
.

In the case of the algebra
\mathcal{A}_{\otimes}=\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\LL})\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\R}}
defined above, its commutant is the algebra
\mathcal{A}_{\otimes}^{\prime}=\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\LL}}\otimes\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\R})
and its centre is simply the commutative algebra
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{A}_{\otimes})=\mathbb{C}\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}}
(the algebra
\mathcal{A}_{\otimes}
is factor). In the case of the algebra
\mathcal{A}_{\oplus}=\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{1})\oplus\mathbb{C}\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{2}}
, its commutant is
\mathcal{A}_{\oplus}^{\prime}=\mathbb{C}\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{1}}\oplus\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{2})
which may seem close to what we get for
\mathcal{A}_{\otimes}^{\prime}
but its centre is the (fundamentally different from
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{A}_{\otimes})
) non-trivial
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{A}_{\oplus})=\mathbb{C}\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{1}}\oplus\mathbb{C}\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{2}}
.

Theorem 2.1

(Atomic projectors)
.

[
47
]

Let
\mathcal{Z}
be a commutative Von Neumann algebra. Then there exists a unique family
\{\pi_{i}\}
of non-null, orthogonal (i.e.
\forall i,\pi_{i}^{\dagger}=\pi_{i}
), and pairwise orthogonal (i.e.
\forall i,j,\pi_{i}\pi_{j}=\delta_{ij}\pi_{i}
) projectors such that
\mathcal{Z}=\Span(\{\pi_{i}\})
. They are called the atomic projectors of
\mathcal{Z}
and we will write
\Atproj(\mathcal{Z})=\{\pi_{i}\}
. For an algebra
\mathcal{A}
that is not necessarily commutative, we will define its atomic projectors as the atomic projectors of its centre, i.e.
\Atproj(\mathcal{A})=\Atproj(\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{A}))
.

Let us now give a representation theorem for Von Neumann algebras which will hopefully make these definitions more concrete in the general case.

Theorem 2.2

(Artin-Wedderburn)
.

[
26
]

Let
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
be a Von Neumann algebra. Then there exists Hilbert spaces
(\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{i})_{i\in I}
and
(\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{i})_{i\in I}
and a unitary map
U:\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\bigoplus_{i}(\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{i}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{i})
such that

U\mathcal{A}U^{\dagger}=\bigoplus_{i}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{i})\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{i}}\,.

(1)

We will call such a unitary a representation unitary for the algebra
\mathcal{A}
.

In the case of the algebra
\mathcal{A}_{\otimes}
defined earlier in this section as acting on the left part of the space
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}_{\LL}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\R}
, this result is completely transparent: the direct sum has a single term and the theorem simply says that
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}_{\LL}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\R}
and that
\mathcal{A}_{\otimes}=\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\LL})\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\R}}
. However in the case of
\mathcal{A}_{\oplus}=\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{1})\oplus\mathbb{C}\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{2}}
, the theorem gives us a non-trivial decomposition. Indeed, it tells us that the underlying space
\mathcal{H}
is (unitarily) isomorphic to
(\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes\mathbb{C}_{1})\oplus(\mathbb{C}_{2}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{2})
and that, following the same mapping,
\mathcal{A}_{\oplus}=\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{1})\oplus\mathbb{C}\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{2}}\cong(\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{1})\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathbb{C}_{1}})\oplus(\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{C}_{2})\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{2}})
.

Remark 2.1
.

Note that the same representation unitary also gives a representation for the algebra
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
as

U\mathcal{A}^{\prime}U^{\dagger}=\bigoplus_{i}\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{i}}\otimes\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{i})\,.

(2)

Remark also that the Artin-Wedderburn theorem makes the atomic projectors appear very naturally. Indeed, if
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
is a Von Neumann algebra and if the Artin-Wedderburn theorem applied to
\mathcal{A}
is stated as

U\mathcal{A}U^{\dagger}=\bigoplus_{i}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{i})\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{i}}\,,

(3)

then the atomic projectors of
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{A})
(and thus of
\mathcal{A}
by extension) are precisely the orthogonal projectors onto the
\mathcal{H}^{i}=U^{\dagger}(\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{i}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{i})
.

Theorem 2.3
.

Let
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
be a Von Neumann algebra. Let
\{\pi_{i}\}
be the family of its atomic projectors and
\mathcal{A}\cong\bigoplus_{i}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{i})\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{i}}
be a decomposition given by Artin-Wedderburn theorem. Then

\mathcal{A}=\bigoplus_{i}\pi_{i}\mathcal{A}\cong\bigoplus_{i}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{i})\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{i}}\cong\bigoplus_{i}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{i})\,,

(4)

meaning in particular that each of the
\pi_{i}\mathcal{A}
is a factor algebra whose center is
\mathcal{Z}(\pi_{i}\mathcal{A})=\mathbb{C}\pi_{i}
.

Identifying
\mathcal{H}
with
\bigoplus_{i}(\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{i}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{i})
, one can now give a notion of partial trace over the algebra
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
. This map from
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
to
\bigoplus_{i}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{i})
that acts as an injective Von Neumann algebra homomorphism on
\mathcal{A}
is essentially an extractor of the algebra
\mathcal{A}
from
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
.

Definition 2.4

(Partial trace over an algebra)
.

[
17
]

The partial trace over the algebra
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
is the map
\Tr_{\mathcal{B}}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})\longrightarrow\bigoplus_{i}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{i})
defined by:

\Tr_{\mathcal{B}}(\cdot):=\bigoplus_{i}\Tr_{\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{i}}(\pi_{i}\cdot\pi_{i})\,,

(5)

where the
\pi_{i}
are the projectors on the
\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{i}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{i}
.

When the algebra considered is
\mathcal{A}_{\otimes}
, we have seen that the decomposition given by Theorem
2.2
is
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}_{\LL}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\R}
and tracing over the algebra
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
meets the usual notion of tracing over the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{\R}
of the factorisation.

In a similar fashion, one can define the trace over the algebra
\mathcal{A}_{\oplus}=\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{1})\oplus\mathbb{C}\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{2}}
. Remark that
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})\cong\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{1})\oplus\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{2})\oplus\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{2},\mathcal{H}_{1})\oplus\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{2})
and let us decompose elements of
A\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
in this way, as
A=A_{1,1}\oplus A_{1,2}\oplus A_{2,1}\oplus A_{2,2}
. Applying Definition
2.4
, we obtain that for any
A\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
,
\Tr_{\mathcal{A}_{\oplus}^{\prime}}(A)=A_{1,1}\oplus\Tr_{\mathcal{H}_{2}}(A_{2,2})\mathbb{1}_{\mathbb{C}_{2}}
, meaning that after tracing we remember about the action of
A
from
\mathcal{H}_{1}
to
\mathcal{H}_{1}
but have lost the information about the proper action of
A
from
\mathcal{H}_{2}
to
\mathcal{H}_{2}
, which has been replaced by
\Tr_{\mathcal{H}_{2}}(A_{2,2})\mathbb{1}_{\mathbb{C}_{2}}
; and we have completely lost track of the action of
A
between
\mathcal{H}_{1}
and
\mathcal{H}_{2}
.

A fundamental information about a family of (sub)systems is the data of whether one of these systems is included in another one, in the sense that it is a subsystem of the latter. This information can be described by a partial order/preorder on the family of systems.

Definition 2.5

(Partial order and preorder)
.

A preorder on a set
X
is a binary relation,
\leq
on
X
that is:

•

reflexive, i.e.
\forall x\in X,x\leq x
,

•

transitive, i.e. if
x\leq y
and
y\leq z
then
x\leq z
.

If
\leq
is moreover

•

antisymmetric, i.e. if
x\leq y
and
y\leq x
then
x=z
,

\leq
is called a partial order.

Proposition 2.1
.

The inclusion between Von Neumann algebras
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
(as it is an inclusion at the level of the sets of elements of the algebras) is a partial order.

In this paper we will be looking for a diagrammatic (as opposed to algebraic) way to reason with general quantum subsystems. In order to do so we aim to find a diagrammatic approach which is suitably equivalent to the algebraic one. The previous proposition tells us that, given a base system, the family of its algebraic subsystems forms a partially ordered set. We will see in Section
3.2
that the family of its diagrammatic subsystems forms a preordered set. The common structure being the one of preordered sets, we define, in order to compare the two approaches, two notions. The first is that of preorder preserving maps, or monotone maps.

Definition 2.6

(Preorder preserving map)
.

Let
(X,\subseteq)
and
(Y,\sqsubseteq)
be preordered sets. We say that a map
f:X\longrightarrow Y
is preorder preserving if, for all
x_{1},x_{2}
in
X
,
x_{1}\subseteq x_{2}\Rightarrow f(x_{1})\sqsubseteq f(x_{2})
.

Second, because preorders can be described as a special kind of categories, we also introduce the notion of equivalence of preorders, which is simply an equivalence of categories
[
35
]
between two preorders.

Definition 2.7

(Equivalence of preorders)
.

Let
(X,\subseteq)
and
(Y,\sqsubseteq)
be preordered sets. We say that the preorder preserving maps
F:X\longrightarrow Y
and
G:Y\longrightarrow X
yield an equivalence of preorders if for all
x\in X
,
x\subseteq G\circ F(x)
and
G\circ F(x)\subseteq x
; and for all
y\in Y
,
y\subseteq F\circ G(y)
and
F\circ G(y)\subseteq y
.

3
Picturing general quantum subsystems as splitting maps

We will now formally define splitting maps: those mathematical objects that will allow us to split systems into a left part and a right part, as well as to define the notions of locality, strict locality, consistency and comprehension that come with this splitting. In particular, we will give a model for subsystems that relies only on the symmetric monoidal structure of the theory at hand, in contrast with the usual algebraic approach, which for instance makes direct reference to closure under sums.
In the rest of this section,
\mathcal{H}
is a fixed Hilbert space.

3.1
Definitions

In the standard paradigm of factor subsystems, the splitting of the system represented by the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
, into subsystems
Y
and
Z
, represented by Hilbert spaces
\mathcal{H}_{Y}
and
\mathcal{H}_{Z}
, amounts to defining a unitary
U:\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{Y}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{Z}
. The idea of splitting maps is to relax this condition and ask for the map
U
to be an isometry instead of a unitary, which will allow us to capture more general subsystems.

Definition 3.1

(Splitting map)
.

A splitting map
\chi
on
\mathcal{H}
is an isometry
\chi:\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{\chi}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{\chi}
where
\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{\chi}
and
\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{\chi}
are Hilbert spaces. We will write it diagrammatically as

\chi
\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{\chi}
\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{\chi}
\mathcal{H}

(6)

and we will write its adjoint
\chi^{\dagger}
, which is a coisometry, as

(7)

We will call
\spl(\mathcal{H})
the set of splitting maps on
\mathcal{H}
.

Let us give a few concrete examples of splitting maps. A first example is the one that recovers the usual decomposition into subsystems and thus the factorisation of a Hilbert space. This happens when the splitting map is not only an isometry, but a unitary
\chi_{\otimes}:\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{\LL}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\R}
, making the tensor product structure of the space appear. Another example of a splitting map can be given in the case of the direct sum. Indeed, if
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{2}
, we have seen that Theorem
2.2
gives us a decomposition of the space as
\mathcal{H}\cong(\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes\mathbb{C}_{1})\oplus(\mathbb{C}_{2}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{2})
which can be naturally embedded into
(\mathcal{H}_{1}\oplus\mathbb{C}_{2})\otimes(\mathbb{C}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{2})=(\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes\mathbb{C}_{1})\oplus(\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{2})\oplus(\mathbb{C}_{2}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{2})\oplus(\mathbb{C}_{2}\otimes\mathbb{C}_{1})
. Naming
\ket{\emptyset_1}
a norm-one element of
\mathbb{C}_{1}
and
\ket{\emptyset_2}
one of
\mathbb{C}_{2}
, we can define this embedding splitting map
\chi_{\oplus}:\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{2}\hookrightarrow(\mathcal{H}_{1}\oplus\mathbb{C}_{2})\otimes(\mathbb{C}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{2})
which, for any
\ket{x}=\ket{x_1}\oplus\ket{x_2}\in\mathcal{H}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{2}=\mathcal{H}
, is such that
\chi_{\oplus}(\ket{x})=\ket{x_1}\otimes\ket{\emptyset_1}+\ket{\emptyset_2}\otimes\ket{x_2}
.

Remark 3.1
.

Remark that
\chi^{\dagger}\chi=\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}}
and that
\chi\chi^{\dagger}
is an orthogonal projector of
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{\chi}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{\chi})
that we will call
\pi^{\chi}
(it is the orthogonal projector on
\imaginary(\chi)
). Diagrammatically we get that

\chi
\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{\chi}
\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{\chi}
\mathcal{H}
\chi
=
\mathcal{H}
\mathcal{H}
\mathcal{H}

(8)

and

\chi
\chi
=
\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{\chi}
\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{\chi}
\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{\chi}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{\chi}
\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{\chi}
\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{\chi}
\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{\chi}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{\chi}
\pi^{\chi}

(9)

This splitting into a left and a right part allows us to talk about operators on
\mathcal{H}
that are (left)-local according to
\chi
.

Definition 3.2

(Locality)
.

Let
\chi
be a splitting map on
\mathcal{H}
and
A\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
. We say that
A
is
\chi
-local if there exists
\tilde{A}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{\chi})
such that
A=\chi^{\dagger}(\tilde{A}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{\chi}})\chi
, or in diagrammatic notation,

(10)

We will say that
\tilde{A}
is an on-site representative of
A
. We will call
\loc(\chi)
the set of
\chi
-local operators of
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
.

Let us now illustrate this definition with the two splitting maps that we defined above. In the case of the tensor splitting
\chi_{\otimes}:\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}_{\LL}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\R}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{\LL}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\R}
, which simply acts as the identity, the local operators are precisely the ones of the form
A=\tilde{A}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\R}}
, i.e. elements of
\mathcal{A}_{\otimes}
, so that

\mathcal{A}_{\otimes}=\loc(\chi_{\otimes})\,.

(11)

Let us now turn to the case of the direct sum and study local operators of the form
A=\chi_{\oplus}^{\dagger}(\tilde{A}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathbb{C}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{2}})\chi_{\oplus}
. Remark that because
\tilde{A}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathbb{C}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{2}}
is trivial on the right part of the splitting,
\tilde{A}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathbb{C}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{2}}(\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes\mathbb{C}_{1})\subseteq(\mathcal{H}_{1}\oplus\mathbb{C}_{2})\otimes\mathbb{C}_{1}
and
\tilde{A}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathbb{C}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{2}}(\mathbb{C}_{2}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{2})\subseteq(\mathcal{H}_{1}\oplus\mathbb{C}_{2})\otimes\mathcal{H}_{2}
. Moreover, as
\chi_{\oplus}
is the map that isometrically embeds
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{2}\cong(\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes\mathbb{C}_{1})\oplus(\mathbb{C}_{2}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{2})
into
(\mathcal{H}_{1}\oplus\mathbb{C}_{2})\otimes(\mathbb{C}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{2})
,
\chi_{\oplus}^{\dagger}
is the map that kills the
\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{2}
and
\mathbb{C}_{2}\otimes\mathbb{C}_{1}
subspaces of
(\mathcal{H}_{1}\oplus\mathbb{C}_{2})\otimes(\mathbb{C}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{2})
and then makes the converse identification with
\mathcal{H}
. It follows that any action of
\tilde{A}\otimes\mathbb{1}
which would move between subspaces
\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes\mathbb{C}_{1}
and
\mathbb{C}_{2}\otimes\mathbb{C}_{1}
or between
\mathbb{C}_{2}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{2}
and
\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{2}
will be killed by the
\chi_{\oplus}
splitting and merging. Therefore,
A
only depends on the diagonal actions
\tilde{A}_{1,1}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{1})
and
\tilde{A}_{2,2}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{C}_{2})\cong\mathbb{C}
(which we will thus call
\alpha_{2}
) of
\tilde{A}
. In the end, the action of
A
on an element
\ket{x}=\ket{x_1}\oplus\ket{x_2}
of
\mathcal{H}
is

\begin{split}A\ket{x}&=\chi_{\oplus}^{\dagger}(\tilde{A}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathbb{C}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{2}})\chi_{\oplus}(\ket{x_1}\oplus\ket{x_2})\\
&=\chi_{\oplus}^{\dagger}(\tilde{A}\ket{x_1}\otimes\ket{\emptyset_1})\oplus\chi_{\oplus}^{\dagger}(\tilde{A}\ket{\emptyset_2}\otimes\ket{x_1})\\
&=\chi_{\oplus}^{\dagger}(\tilde{A}_{1,1}\ket{x_1}\otimes\ket{\emptyset_1})\oplus\chi_{\oplus}^{\dagger}(\tilde{A}_{2,2}\ket{\emptyset_2}\otimes\ket{x_1})\\
&=\tilde{A}_{1,1}\ket{x_1}\oplus\alpha_{2}\ket{x_2}\,,\end{split}

(12)

which directly implies that
A
is an element of the algebra
\mathcal{A}_{\oplus}
defined earlier. Conversely, any element of
\mathcal{A}_{\oplus}
is a
\chi_{\oplus}
-local operator, by taking its action on
\mathcal{H}_{1}
as
\tilde{A}_{1,1}
and its homothety ratio on
\mathcal{H}_{2}
as
\tilde{A}_{2,2}=\alpha_{2}
. Thus, here as well,

\mathcal{A}_{\oplus}=\loc(\chi_{\oplus})\,.

(13)

However, while in these examples the
\chi
-local operators already form a Von Neumann algebra, this is not the case in general.

Remark 3.2
.

Local operators usually only have the structure of an operator system, i.e.  a linear subspace of
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
that is closed under the dagger and contains the identity, but that is not necessarily closed under composition. And conversely any operator system is the image of a unital completely positive map
[
53
]
and can thus be viewed as the local operators of any Stinespring dilation of this map.

The fact that local operators were not forming an algebra was already remarked in
[
5
]
, where an additional requirement on
\chi
was proposed as a remedy. The following definition generalizes this idea.

Definition 3.3

(
\chi
-consistency)
.

Let
\chi
be a splitting map on
\mathcal{H}
. We will say that an on-site operator
B\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{\chi})
is
\chi
-consistent if
\pi^{\chi}(B\otimes\mathbb{1})=(B\otimes\mathbb{1})\pi^{\chi}
, or in diagrammatic notation,

\hss}\lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\,.

(14)

We will call
\cons(\chi)
the set of
\chi
-consistent operators of
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{\chi})
.

This property is equivalent to asking that
\imaginary(\chi)
and
\imaginary(\chi)^{\perp}
are invariant for
B\otimes\mathbb{1}
.

Proposition 3.1
.

Let
\chi
be a splitting map on
\mathcal{H}
and
B\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{\chi})
,
B
is
\chi
-consistent if and only if
(B\otimes\mathbb{1})(\imaginary(\chi))\subseteq\imaginary(\chi)
and
(B\otimes\mathbb{1})(\imaginary(\chi)^{\perp})\subseteq\imaginary(\chi)^{\perp}
.

Proof.

Let
\chi
be a splitting map on
\mathcal{H}
and
B\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{\chi})
. Suppose that
B
is
\chi
-consistent and let
\ket{x}\in\imaginary(\chi)
and
\ket{y}\in\imaginary(\chi)^{\perp}
. We have that
(B\otimes\mathbb{1})\ket{x}=(B\otimes\mathbb{1})\pi^{\chi}\ket{x}=\pi^{\chi}(B\otimes\mathbb{1})\in\imaginary(\pi^{\chi})=\imaginary(\chi)
and that
\pi^{\chi}(B\otimes\mathbb{1})\ket{y}=(B\otimes\mathbb{1})\pi^{\chi}\ket{y}=0
, which implies that
(B\otimes\mathbb{1})\ket{y}\in\imaginary(\pi^{\chi})^{\perp}=\imaginary(\chi)^{\perp}
. Conversely, suppose that
(B\otimes\mathbb{1})(\imaginary(\chi))\subseteq\imaginary(\chi)
and
(B\otimes\mathbb{1})(\imaginary(\chi)^{\perp})\subseteq\imaginary(\chi)^{\perp}
. Because
\imaginary(\chi)
and
\imaginary(\chi)^{\perp}
span
\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{\chi}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{\chi}
, it is sufficient to show that the equality holds on these subspaces which is immediate, in a similar way to the direct case.
∎

Definition 3.4

(Strict locality)
.

Let
\chi
be a splitting map on
\mathcal{H}
and
A\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
. We say that
A
is strictly
\chi
-local if there exists
\tilde{A}\subset\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{\chi})
such that
A\chi^{\dagger}=\chi^{\dagger}(\tilde{A}\otimes\mathbb{1})
and
\chi A=(\tilde{A}\otimes\mathbb{1})\chi
, or diagrammatically,

(15)

We will call
\stloc(\chi)
the set of strictly
\chi
-local operators of
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
.

In the case of the tensor splitting
\chi_{\otimes}:\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}_{\LL}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\R}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{\LL}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\R}
, acting as the identity, the definition reduces to:
A
is strictly local if and only if
A=\tilde{A}\otimes\mathbb{1}
, which implies that

\stloc(\chi_{\otimes})=\loc(\chi_{\otimes})=\mathcal{A}_{\otimes}\,.

(16)

Let us now look at
\stloc(\chi_{\oplus})
. Let
A
(which we can write
A=A_{1,1}\oplus A_{1,2}\oplus A_{2,1}\oplus A_{2,2}
according to the decomposition of the underlying space
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{2}
) be a strictly
\chi_{\oplus}
-local operator. The equality
\chi_{\oplus}A=(\tilde{A}\otimes\mathbb{1})\chi_{\oplus}
tells us that fot any
\ket{x}=\ket{x_1}\oplus\ket{x_2}
, we have
(A_{1,1}\ket{x_1}+A_{2,1}\ket{x_2})\otimes\ket{\emptyset_1})+(\ket{\emptyset_2}\otimes(A_{1,2}\ket{x_1}+A_{2,2}\ket{x_2}))=(\tilde{A}\ket{x_1}\otimes\ket{\emptyset_1})+(\tilde{A}\ket{\emptyset_2}\otimes\ket{x_2})
, which implies that
A_{1,2}=0
and
A_{2,1}=0
; it follows that
A\in\mathcal{A}_{\oplus}
. Conversely, any element of
\mathcal{A}_{\oplus}
is a strictly
\chi_{\oplus}
-local operator: this can be witnessed by taking
\tilde{A}=\tilde{A}_{1}\oplus\tilde{A}_{2}
where
\tilde{A}_{1}
is its action on
\mathcal{H}_{1}
and
\tilde{A}_{2}=\alpha_{2}
is its homothety ratio on
\mathcal{H}_{2}
. Therefore we can conclude that

\stloc(\chi_{\oplus})=\loc(\chi_{\oplus})=\mathcal{A}_{\oplus}\,.

(17)

Remark that in these particular examples, the sets of local and strictly local operators were equal, and also that the two equations of the definition of strictly local operators were redundant. These two fact are not true in general but are degeneracies due to the fact that
\chi_{\otimes}
and
\chi_{\oplus}
belong to a special class of splitting maps that we will call balanced and study in Section
4.3
.

Proposition 3.2
.

Let
\chi
be a splitting map on
\mathcal{H}
and
A\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
.
A
is strictly
\chi
-local if and only if there exists a
\chi
-consistent
\tilde{A}
such that
A=\chi^{\dagger}(\tilde{A}\otimes\mathbb{1})\chi
, i.e. if it is local and has a
\chi
-consistent on-site representative.

Proof.

We will prove this proposition diagrammatically. Suppose that
A
is strictly
\chi
-local; then it is local as

(19)

-consistent, such that

(20)

and the other equality is proved symmetrically.
∎

Remark 3.3
.

Remark that we have more or less implicitly defined the notions of
\chi
-locality,
\chi
-consistency, strict
\chi
-locality on the left branch of the isometry. But symmetric definitions can be given on the right branch of the isometry and they satisfy all the properties that we have proved for the former. If we don’t state otherwise, we will keep talking about the left branch but if what happens on both branches comes to play we will then talk about left
\chi
-local operators, denoted
\loc_{\LL}(\chi)
, and right
\chi
-local operators, denoted
\loc_{\R}(\chi)
(and about other notions in a similar fashion).

3.2
Comprehension : a preorder for splitting maps

While we have defined splitting maps as isometries that split a system into a left part and a right part, it is also possible to understand them as objects that extract the subsystem associated to the left branch from the global system. It then becomes very natural to wonder whether one can define some kind of order relation on splitting maps that would eventually match the idea that a subsystem (extracted by the left branch of
\zeta
) is included in another (extracted by the left branch of
\chi
). We thus define the following notion of inclusion between between splitting maps that we will call comprehension.

Definition 3.5

(Comprehension)
.

Let
\chi
and
\zeta
be two splitting maps on
\mathcal{H}
. We say that
\zeta
is comprehended in
\chi
and write
\zeta\sqsubseteq\chi
if there exists a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{\MM}
and isometries
\hbox to8.4pt{\vbox to8.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.2pt\lower-4.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 5.53 0 C 5.53 3.06 3.06 5.53 0 5.53 C -3.06 5.53 -5.53 3.06 -5.53 0 C -5.53 -3.06 -3.06 -5.53 0 -5.53 C 3.06 -5.53 5.53 -3.06 5.53 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}:\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{\zeta}\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}_{\MM}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{\chi}
and
\bigcirc:\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{\chi}\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{\zeta}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\MM}
such that
(\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{\zeta}}\otimes\hbox to8.4pt{\vbox to8.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.2pt\lower-4.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}})\zeta=(\bigcirc\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{\chi}})\chi
, or diagrammatically,

(21)

Proposition 3.3
.

Comprehension is a preorder relation on the set of splitting maps on
\mathcal{H}
.

Proof.

Comprehension is reflexive as for any splitting map
\chi
on
\mathcal{H}
, we can choose
\mathcal{H}_{\MM}=\mathbb{C}
,
\hbox to8.4pt{\vbox to8.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.2pt\lower-4.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}

and
\bigcirc:y\longrightarrow y\otimes 1
and get that
(\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{\zeta}}\otimes\hbox to8.4pt{\vbox to8.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.2pt\lower-4.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}

(23)

and the conclusion is immediate by taking
\mathcal{H}_{\MM}=\mathcal{H}_{{\MM}_{1}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{{\MM}_{2}}
and for isometries the compositions of dot isometries as above.
∎

4
Equivalence between diagrammatic and algebraic subsystems

4.1
From diagrammatic subsystems to algebraic subsystems

Let us now prove a few structural properties of these notions, which will allow us to link splitting maps to Von Neumann algebras.

Definition 4.1
.

We define
\sigma^{\chi}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{\chi})\longrightarrow\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
as
\sigma^{\chi}(B)=\chi^{\dagger}(B\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{\chi}})\chi
.

(24)

It is a linear operator such that
\sigma^{\chi}(A^{\dagger})=\sigma^{\chi}(A)^{\dagger}
.
Moreover it satisfies

•

\loc(\chi)=\sigma^{\chi}(\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{\chi}))

•

\stloc(\chi)=\sigma^{\chi}(\cons(\chi))

Proposition 4.1
.

The set of
\chi
-consistent operators,
\cons(\chi)
, is a Von Neumann algebra included in
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{\chi})
. It is the biggest Von Neumann algebra
\mathcal{A}
in
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{\chi})
such that
\sigma^{\chi}_{|\mathcal{A}}
is a
*
-algebra homomorphism.

Proof.

First, let us remark that
\cons(\chi)
is a linear subspace of
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{\chi})
containing
\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{\chi}}
; furthermore it is stable by composition since for all
A,B
in
\cons(\chi)
,
\pi^{\chi}(AB\otimes\mathbb{1})=(A\otimes\mathbb{1})\pi^{\chi}(B\otimes\mathbb{1})=(AB\otimes\mathbb{1})\pi^{\chi}
; finally it is stable by adjoint as for all
A
in
\cons(\chi)
,
\pi^{\chi}(A^{\dagger}\otimes\mathbb{1})=((A\otimes\mathbb{1})\pi^{\chi})^{\dagger}=(\pi^{\chi}(A\otimes\mathbb{1}))^{\dagger}=(A^{\dagger}\otimes\mathbb{1})\pi^{\chi}
. This proves that
\cons(\chi)
is a Von Neumann subalgebra of
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{\chi})
. Let us now prove that
\sigma^{\chi}_{|\mathcal{A}}
is a
*
-algebra homomorphism. It is linear and preserves the adjoint as
\chi^{\dagger}(A^{\dagger}\otimes\mathbb{1})\chi=(\chi^{\dagger}(A\otimes\mathbb{1})\chi)^{\dagger}
. It remains to show that it preserves the product, which we can show diagrammatically as

{\hbox to292.91pt{\vbox to214.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.5764pt\lower-89.41487pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \hbox to0.0pt{
{
{}{}{}}{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{{}}{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}
{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{{}}{}}{{{}}{{}}}{{}}{}{{}}{{{{{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}}}}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 4.92 C 0 -18.23 3.29 -34.45 26.45 -34.45}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{

(25)

Finally let us prove that it is the biggest Von Neumann algebra
\mathcal{A}
of
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{\chi})
such that
\sigma^{\chi}_{|\mathcal{A}}
is a
*
-algebra homomorphism. Let
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{\chi})
be a Von Neumann algebra satisfying this property. Let
A\in\mathcal{A}
and
\overline{\pi^{\chi}}=\mathbb{1}-\pi^{\chi}
. Then
\pi^{\chi}(A\otimes\mathbb{1})^{\dagger}(A\otimes\mathbb{1})\pi^{\chi}=\pi^{\chi}(A\otimes\mathbb{1})^{\dagger}\pi^{\chi}(A\otimes\mathbb{1})\pi^{\chi}
, thus
\parallel(A\otimes\mathbb{1})\pi^{\chi}\parallel^{2}=\Tr(\pic(A \otimes\id)^{\dagger} (A \otimes\id) \pic)=\Tr(\pic(A \otimes\id)^{\dagger} \pic(A \otimes\id) \pic)=\parallel\pi^{\chi}(A\otimes\mathbb{1})\pi^{\chi}\parallel^{2}
and because
(A\otimes\mathbb{1})\pi^{\chi}=\pi^{\chi}(A\otimes\mathbb{1})\pi^{\chi}+\overline{\pi^{\chi}}(A\otimes\mathbb{1})\pi^{\chi}
with
\langle\pi^{\chi}(A\otimes\mathbb{1})\pi^{\chi},\overline{\pi^{\chi}}(A\otimes\mathbb{1})\pi^{\chi}\rangle=0
, by the Pythagorean theorem
\parallel(A\otimes\mathbb{1})\pi^{\chi}\parallel^{2}=\parallel\pi^{\chi}(A\otimes\mathbb{1})\pi^{\chi}\parallel^{2}+\parallel\overline{\pi^{\chi}}(A\otimes\mathbb{1})\pi^{\chi}\parallel^{2}
which implies that
\parallel\overline{\pi^{\chi}}(A\otimes\mathbb{1})\pi^{\chi}\parallel^{2}=0
and thus that
\overline{\pi^{\chi}}(A\otimes\mathbb{1})\pi^{\chi}=0
. This tells us that
(A\otimes\mathbb{1})\pi^{\chi}=\pi^{\chi}(A\otimes\mathbb{1})\pi^{\chi}
. The same reasoning can be done with
\Tr(\pic(A \otimes\id) (A \otimes\id)^{\dagger} \pic)=\Tr(\pic(A \otimes\id) \pic(A \otimes\id)^{\dagger} \pic)
; it follows that
\pi^{\chi}(A\otimes\mathbb{1})=\pi^{\chi}(A\otimes\mathbb{1})\pi^{\chi}
, which concludes the proof.
∎

Corollary 4.1
.

The set of strictly
\chi
-local operators,
\stloc(\chi)
, is a Von Neumann subalgebra of
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
.

This result tells us that, starting from a splitting map, we can recover an algebraic subsystem as its set of strictly local operators. Let us now show that comprehension between splitting maps implies the respective inclusions of their local and strictly-local operators.

Proposition 4.2
.

Let
\chi
and
\zeta
be two splitting maps on
\mathcal{H}
. If
\zeta\sqsubseteq\chi
, then:

1.

\loc(\zeta)\subseteq\loc(\chi)

2.

\stloc(\zeta)\subseteq\stloc(\chi)

Proof.

Let
A\in\loc(\zeta)
. Writing

\zeta
\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{\zeta}
\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{\zeta}
\mathcal{H}
\tilde{A}
\chi
A
=
\mathcal{H}
=
\zeta
\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{\zeta}
\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{\zeta}
\mathcal{H}
\tilde{A}
\chi
\mathcal{H}
=
\chi
\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{\zeta}
\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{\chi}
\mathcal{H}
\tilde{A}
\chi
\mathcal{H}
\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{\chi}
\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{\chi}

(26)

proves that
A\in\loc(\chi)
and thus that
\loc(\zeta)\subseteq\loc(\chi)
. Suppose now that
\tilde{A}\in\cons(\zeta)
and thus that
A\in\stloc(\zeta)
, we get that

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-99.58466pt}{-128.03741pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-156.49017pt}{-128.03741pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\hss}\lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
}}\,,

(27)

which proves that
\bigcirc^{\dagger}(\tilde{A}\otimes\mathbb{1})\bigcirc\in\cons(\chi)
and thus that
A\in\stloc(\chi)
.
∎

As a consequence of these results, we have that the assignment of the set of strictly-local operators to any splitting map gives a preorder preserving map from splitting maps to Von Neumann algebras,

\stloc(\cdot):(\spl(\mathcal{H}),\sqsubseteq)\longrightarrow(\vnalg(\mathcal{H}),\subseteq).

This in-turn sets up one-half of a possible equivalence between Von Neumann algebras and splitting maps.

4.2
From algebraic subsystems to diagrammatic subsystems

In the previous sections, we defined splitting maps as a tool to select a subsystem and showed that we can associate to each a Von Neumann subalgebra of
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
, through the preorder preserving map
\stloc(\cdot):(\spl(\mathcal{H}),\sqsubseteq)\longrightarrow(\vnalg(\mathcal{H}),\subseteq)
. In this section we will first look at the reverse direction and show that we can associate to any Von Neumann subalgebra of
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
a canonical splitting map, provided by the Artin-Wedderburn theorem, and for which locality and strict locality coincide.

Proposition 4.3
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a unital subalgebra of
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
and
U:\mathcal{H}\longrightarrow\bigoplus_{i}(\mathcal{H}_{{\LL}}^{i}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{{\R}}^{i})
be a representation unitary for
\mathcal{A}
. Then

\displaystyle\chi_{U}:

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}\longrightarrow\bigoplus_{i}(\mathcal{H}_{{\LL}}^{i}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{{\R}}^{i})\hookrightarrow(\oplus_{i}\mathcal{H}_{{\LL}}^{i})\otimes(\oplus_{i}\mathcal{H}_{{\R}}^{i})

(28)

\displaystyle\ket{x}\longrightarrow U(\ket{x})

(29)

is a splitting map such that
\stloc_{\LL}(\chi_{U})=\loc_{\LL}(\chi_{U})=\mathcal{A}
and
\stloc_{\R}(\chi_{U})=\loc_{\R}(\chi_{U})=\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
. We will call
\chi
a canonical splitting map for
\mathcal{A}
.

Proof.

First we remark that
\imaginary(\chi_{U})=\bigoplus_{i}(\mathcal{H}_{{\LL}}^{i}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{{\R}}^{i})\subseteq(\oplus_{i}\mathcal{H}_{{\LL}}^{i})\otimes(\oplus_{i}\mathcal{H}_{{\R}}^{i})
and thus that
\pi^{\chi_{U}}=\bigoplus_{i}\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{{\LL}}^{i}}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{{\R}}^{i}}
, as it is the projector on
\imaginary(\chi_{U})
. Then the left
\chi_{U}
-consistent operators are the operators
A
of
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\chi_{U}}^{{\LL}})=\mathcal{L}(\bigoplus_{i}\mathcal{H}_{{\LL}}^{i})
such that
A\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\bigoplus_{i}\mathcal{H}_{{\R}}^{i}}
commutes with
\pi^{\chi_{U}}=\bigoplus_{i}\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{{\LL}}^{i}}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{{\R}}^{i}}
. This means that
\bigoplus_{i}(A\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{{\LL}}^{i}})\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{{\R}}^{i}}=\bigoplus_{i}(\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{{\LL}}^{i}}A)\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{{\R}}^{i}}
and thus that for all
i
,
A\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{{\LL}}^{i}}=\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{{\LL}}^{i}}A=\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{{\LL}}^{i}}A\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{{\LL}}^{i}}
. It follows, because the
\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{{\LL}}^{i}}
are a family of pairwise orthogonal projectors that sum to
\mathbb{1}_{\bigoplus_{i}\mathcal{H}_{{\LL}}^{i}}
, that
A=(\mathbb{1}_{\bigoplus_{i}\mathcal{H}_{{\LL}}^{i}})A(\mathbb{1}_{\bigoplus_{i}\mathcal{H}_{{\LL}}^{i}})=\bigoplus_{i}\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{{\LL}}^{i}}A\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{{\LL}}^{i}}=\bigoplus_{i}A_{i}
with
A_{i}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{{\LL}}^{i})
. Conversely, one can check that all operators of this form are left
\chi_{U}
-consistent.

We then compute that
\sigma^{\chi_{U}}(A)=\chi_{U}^{\dagger}(A\otimes\mathbb{1})\chi_{U}=\chi_{U}^{\dagger}\pi^{\chi_{U}}(A\otimes\mathbb{1})\pi^{\chi_{U}}\chi_{U}=\chi_{U}^{\dagger}(\bigoplus_{i}\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{i}}A\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{i}}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{i}})\chi_{U}
, which we can write as
U^{\dagger}(\bigoplus_{i}A_{i}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{{\R}}^{i}})U
. We therefore get that
\loc(\chi_{U})=\sigma^{\chi_{U}}(\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{\chi})=\sigma^{\chi_{U}}(\cons_{\LL}(\chi))=\stloc(\chi_{U})\subseteq\mathcal{A}
. Conversely, any element of
\mathcal{A}
can be decomposed like this because all maps of the form
\bigoplus_{i}A_{i}
with
A_{i}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{{\LL}}^{i})
are left
\chi_{U}
-consistent. This proves that
\stloc_{\LL}(\chi_{U})=\loc_{\LL}(\chi_{U})=\mathcal{A}
. A symmetric reasoning proves that
\stloc_{\R}(\chi_{U})=\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
.
∎

Remark 4.1
.

A given subalgebra
\mathcal{A}
of
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
doesn’t have a unique canonical splitting map, but all its canonical splitting maps are equal up to some local unitaries on the left and on the right.

Let us now show that a map
F:(\vnalg(\mathcal{H}),\subseteq)\rightarrow(\spl(\mathcal{H}),\sqsubseteq)
that sends every
\mathcal{A}\in\vnalg(\mathcal{H})
to a canonical splitting map
\chi_{\mathcal{A}}
will be preorder preserving.

Proposition 4.4
.

Let
\mathcal{A}_{S}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{B}
be two Von Neumann subalgebras of
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
. Let
\chi:\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{B}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{B}
and
\zeta:\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{S}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{S}
be canonical splitting maps for
\mathcal{A}_{B}
and
\mathcal{A}_{S}
respectively. Then
\zeta\sqsubseteq\chi
.

Proof.

See Appendix
B
.
∎

As a result of the above we have established the following: that there is a preorder homomorphism from Von Neumann algebras to splitting maps, and that there is an inverse preorder homomorphism from splitting maps to Von Neumann algebras.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\vnalg(\mathcal{H}),\subseteq)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\spl(\mathcal{H}),\sqsubseteq)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G=\stloc(.)}

One might wonder whether this pair of preorder homomorphisms establishes an equivalence, so that
FG=id
. In fact, it is not even true that for each
\chi
,
FG(\chi)\cong\chi
. Indeed, a simple counter example is the following. Consider
\chi:\mathbb{C}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{4}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{4}
such that
\chi\ket{0}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\ket{00}+\ket{11})
and
\chi\ket{1}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\ket{20}+\ket{33})
. Its algebra of strictly (left)-local operators is
\stloc_{\LL}(\chi)=\{\alpha\outerproduct{0}{0}+\beta\outerproduct{1}{1}\}\subseteq\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{C}^{2})
and thus
\zeta=FG(\chi)
will be a canonical splitting map such that
\zeta\ket{0}=\ket{00}
and
\zeta\ket{1}=\ket{11}
. Now suppose that
\chi\sqsubseteq\zeta
and thus that
(\mathbb{1}\otimes\hbox to8.4pt{\vbox to8.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.2pt\lower-4.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 5.53 0 C 5.53 3.06 3.06 5.53 0 5.53 C -3.06 5.53 -5.53 3.06 -5.53 0 C -5.53 -3.06 -3.06 -5.53 0 -5.53 C 3.06 -5.53 5.53 -3.06 5.53 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}})\chi=(\bigcirc\otimes\mathbb{1})\zeta
. Applying this equality to
\ket{0}
, we find that
\hbox to8.4pt{\vbox to8.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.2pt\lower-4.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 5.53 0 C 5.53 3.06 3.06 5.53 0 5.53 C -3.06 5.53 -5.53 3.06 -5.53 0 C -5.53 -3.06 -3.06 -5.53 0 -5.53 C 3.06 -5.53 5.53 -3.06 5.53 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\ket{0}=\ket{m_0}\otimes\ket{0}
, and applying it to
\ket{1}
yields
\hbox to8.4pt{\vbox to8.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.2pt\lower-4.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 5.53 0 C 5.53 3.06 3.06 5.53 0 5.53 C -3.06 5.53 -5.53 3.06 -5.53 0 C -5.53 -3.06 -3.06 -5.53 0 -5.53 C 3.06 -5.53 5.53 -3.06 5.53 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\ket{0}=\ket{m_1}\otimes\ket{1}
. This is not possible and thus contradicts the fact that
\chi\sqsubseteq\zeta=FG(\chi)
.

Nonetheless, it is natural to wonder which splitting maps satisfy the property that
FG(\chi)\cong\chi
, since keeping only those splitting maps would establish the widest possible equivalence (based on strictly local operators) between splitting maps and Von Neumann algebras.

Remark 4.2
.

If
\chi
is a splitting map such that
FG(\chi)\cong\chi
, i.e.
\chi\sqsubseteq G\circ F(\chi)\sqsubseteq\chi
, then by definition of
G
,
\zeta=G\circ F(\chi)
is a canonical splitting map for the algebra
\mathcal{A}=\stloc_{\LL}(\chi)
and thus such that
\stloc_{\LL}(\zeta)=\stloc_{\R}(\zeta)^{\prime}
. Then because
\chi\sqsubseteq\zeta
,
\stloc_{\LL}(\chi)\subseteq\stloc_{\LL}(\zeta)
and
\stloc_{\R}(\zeta)\subseteq\stloc_{\R}(\chi)
. And because
\zeta\sqsubseteq\chi
,
\stloc_{\LL}(\zeta)\subseteq\stloc_{\LL}(\chi)
and
\stloc_{\R}(\chi)\subseteq\stloc_{\R}(\zeta)
. It follows that
\stloc_{\LL}(\chi)=\stloc_{\LL}(\zeta)
and that
\stloc_{\R}(\zeta)=\stloc_{\R}(\chi)
and then that

\stloc_{\LL}(\chi)=\stloc_{\R}(\chi)^{\prime}.

This leads naturally to the question: can those
\chi
that are balanced in the above sense be viewed as equivalent up to preorder to Von Neumann algebras?

4.3
Balanced splitting maps

We will focus in this section on the class of these balanced splitting maps. First, let us remark that this is not a trivial requirement: in general, the strictly left local operators and strictly right local operators of a splitting map only have to be commuting subalgebras, and not precisely each other’s commutant. Indeed, let us consider the splitting map
\chi:\mathbb{C}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2}
defined by
\chi(\ket{0})=\ket{00}
and
\chi(\ket{1})=\ket{10}
: it leads to
\stloc_{\LL}(\chi)=\{\alpha\outerproduct{0}{0}+\beta\outerproduct{1}{1}\}
and
\stloc_{\R}(\chi)=\mathbb{C}\mathbb{1}
, which are not each other’s commutants as
\stloc_{\LL}(\chi)^{\prime}=\stloc_{\LL}(\chi)\neq\stloc_{\R}(\chi)
.

We will start by defining balanced splitting maps, then show that they can be decomposed in a way that is related to canonical splitting maps. Finally we will prove that they yield the wanted equivalence with Von Neumann algebras.

Definition 4.2

(Balanced splitting maps)
.

A splitting map
\chi
is said to be balanced if
\stloc_{\R}(\chi)=\stloc_{\LL}(\chi)^{\prime}
. We will call
\balanced(\mathcal{H})
the set of balanced splitting maps on
\mathcal{H}
.

We also define a subclass of balanced splitting maps that we will call lean and that are encoding the algebras of strictly local operators in a minimal way, i.e. such that the corresponding on-site algebras of consistent operators have no redundancies.

Definition 4.3

(Lean splitting maps)
.

A splitting map is said to be lean if it is balanced and its algebras of
\chi
-consistent operators are such that
\cons_{\LL}(\chi)^{\prime}=\mathcal{Z}(\cons_{\LL}(\chi))\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{\chi}
and
\cons_{\R}(\chi)^{\prime}=\mathcal{Z}(\cons_{\R}(\chi))\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{\chi}
. We will call
\lean(\mathcal{H})
the set of lean splitting maps on
\mathcal{H}
.

We will now give a general result on the decomposition of splitting maps. It will allow us to reconstruct the shape of balanced splitting maps from the structure of the ones whose algebras of strictly local operators are factors.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
\chi\in\spl(\mathcal{H})
and let
\Atproj(\mathcal{Z}(\stloc_{\LL}(\chi)))=\{\pi_{i}\}_{i\in I}
. Then there exists a family of Hilbert spaces
\{\mathcal{H}_{i}=\pi_{i}\mathcal{H}\}_{i\in I}
and isometries
\chi_{i}=\chi\pi_{i}:\mathcal{H}\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{\chi}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{\chi}
such that:

•

\mathcal{H}=\bigoplus_{i}\mathcal{H}_{i}
,

•

\chi=\sum_{i}\chi_{i}=\sum_{i}(\chi_{i})_{|\mathcal{H}_{i}}
,

•

for all
i
in
I
,
(\chi_{i})_{|\mathcal{H}_{i}}
is a splitting map on
\mathcal{H}_{i}
,

•

its algebra of strictly local operators (seen as a subalgebra of
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
) is
\pi_{i}\stloc_{\LL}(\chi)
and is thus a factor,

•

there exists a family
\{\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{i}\}
of pairwise orthogonal subspaces of
\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{\chi}
such that
\imaginary(\chi_{i})\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{i}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{\chi}
.

Proof.

The first two points are immediate because the atomic projectors of an algebra sum to the identity. Then, because the
\pi_{i}
are orthogonal projectors, they are isometries when restricted to their image and thus
(\chi_{i})_{|\mathcal{H}_{i}}=(\chi\pi_{i})_{|\mathcal{H}_{i}}=\chi(\pi_{i})_{|\mathcal{H}_{i}}
is a splitting map.

Let us now prove that its algebra of strictly local operators is
\pi_{i}\stloc_{\LL}(\chi)
. Let
A\in\cons_{\LL}(\chi)
, we get that
\sigma^{\chi}(A)=\chi^{\dagger}(A\otimes\mathbb{1})\chi\in\stloc_{\LL}(\chi)
commutes with
\pi_{i}
by definition of the atomic projectors. It follows that
\chi\chi^{\dagger}(A\otimes\mathbb{1})\chi\pi_{i}\chi^{\dagger}=\chi\pi_{i}\chi^{\dagger}(A\otimes\mathbb{1})\chi\chi^{\dagger}
and then that
(A\otimes\mathbb{1})\chi\pi_{i}\chi^{\dagger}=\chi\pi_{i}\chi^{\dagger}(A\otimes\mathbb{1})
because
A
is left
\chi
-consistent. Moreover, remark that
\chi_{i}\chi_{i}^{\dagger}=\chi\pi_{i}\chi^{\dagger}
and thus
A
is left
\chi_{i}
-consistent. It follows that
\pi_{i}\sigma^{\chi}(A)=\sigma^{\chi_{i}}(A)\in\stloc_{\LL}(\chi_{i})
, which proves that
\pi_{i}\stloc_{\LL}(\chi)\subseteq\stloc_{\LL}(\chi_{i})
.

It remains to show the reverse inclusion. By Proposition
A.1
, we know that there exists an orthogonal projector
\mu
(sum of atomic projectors of
\cons_{\LL}(\chi)
) such that
\sigma^{\chi}_{|\mu\cons_{\LL}(\chi)}:\mu\cons_{\LL}(\chi)\longrightarrow\stloc_{\LL}(\chi)
is an isomorphism of Von Neumann algebras. In particular it is such that the
\tilde{\pi}_{i}=(\sigma^{\chi}_{|\mu\cons_{\LL}(\chi)})^{-1}(\pi_{i})
are atomic projectors of
\cons_{\LL}(\chi)
(they’re precisely the ones that are not killed by
\sigma^{\chi}
). Let
A\in\cons_{\LL}(\chi_{i})
, i.e. such that
(A\otimes\mathbb{1})\chi\pi_{i}\chi^{\dagger}=\chi\pi_{i}\chi^{\dagger}(A\otimes\mathbb{1})
. By definition of the
\tilde{\pi}_{i}
, this implies that
(A\otimes\mathbb{1})\chi\chi^{\dagger}(\tilde{\pi}_{i}\otimes\mathbb{1})\chi\chi^{\dagger}=\chi\chi^{\dagger}(\tilde{\pi}_{i}\otimes\mathbb{1})\chi\chi^{\dagger}(A\otimes\mathbb{1})
and then that
(\tilde{\pi}_{i}A\otimes\mathbb{1})\chi\chi^{\dagger}=\chi\chi^{\dagger}(\tilde{\pi}_{i}A\otimes\mathbb{1})
meaning that
\tilde{\pi}_{i}A
is left
\chi
-consistent. It follows that
\sigma^{\chi_{i}}(A)=\chi_{i}^{\dagger}(A\otimes\mathbb{1})\chi_{i}=\pi_{i}\chi^{\dagger}(A\otimes\mathbb{1})\chi\pi_{i}=\pi_{i}\chi^{\dagger}(\tilde{\pi}_{i}A\otimes\mathbb{1})\chi=\pi_{i}\sigma^{\chi}(\tilde{\pi}_{i}A)\in\pi_{i}\stloc_{\LL}(\chi)
proving the fourth point.

Let us finally prove the last point. By definition the
\tilde{\pi_{i}}
are
\chi
-consistent on-site representatives of the
\pi_{i}\in\stloc_{\LL}(\chi)
, which yields that for all
i
,
\chi_{i}=\chi\pi_{i}=(\tilde{\pi_{i}}\otimes id)\chi
. Then, as we have seen, the
\tilde{\pi_{i}}
are atomic projectors of the algebra
\cons_{\LL}(\chi)
and are thus pairwise orthogonal projectors. Taking
\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{i}=\imaginary(\tilde{\pi_{i}})
concludes the proof.

∎

Remark that a similar “decomposition”can be done with respect to
\stloc_{\R}(\chi)
and that it will in general be different from the first one, because the two algebras have different atomic projectors. However, note that when
\chi
is balanced, i.e. when
\stloc_{\LL}(\chi)=\stloc_{\R}(\chi)^{\prime}
, the two algebras share the same atomic projectors and thus the two decompositions are the same. This allows us to infer the shape of general balanced splitting maps from the ones whose algebras of strictly local operators are factor. Let us thus focus on the latter ones.

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
\chi\in\balanced(\mathcal{H})
and suppose that
\mathcal{A}=\stloc_{\LL}(\chi)
is factor. Then there exists an orthonormal basis
\{\ket{lr}\}
of
\mathcal{H}
and families of orthonormal elements
\{\ket{l+m}\}
in
\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{\chi}
and
\{\ket{r+m}\}
in
\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{\chi}
such that
\ket{\phi_{lr}}=\chi(\ket{lr})=\sum_{m}\lambda_{m}\ket{l+m}\ket{r+m}
.

Proof.

First remember that by Theorem
2.2
there exists a unitary isomorphism
\mathcal{H}\cong\bigoplus_{i}\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{i}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{i}
such that
\mathcal{A}\cong\bigoplus_{i}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{i})\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{i}}
(and symmetrically for
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
). Because
\mathcal{A}
is a factor, this sum has a unique term
\mathcal{H}\cong\mathcal{H}_{\LL}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\R}
and
\mathcal{A}\cong\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\LL})\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\R}}
. Let’s make a choice of orthonormal basis
\{\ket{l }\}
of
\mathcal{H}_{\LL}
and
\{\ket{r }\}
of
\mathcal{H}_{\R}
; for the rest of the proof we will identify
\mathcal{H}
with
\mathcal{H}_{\LL}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\R}
equipped with the orthonormal basis
\{\ket{l}\otimes\ket{r}\}
. Then by Proposition
A.1
there exists an orthogonal projector
\mu\in\cons_{\LL}(\chi)
such that
\sigma^{\chi}_{|\mu(\cons_{\LL}(\chi))}:\mu(\cons_{\LL}(\chi))\longrightarrow\stloc(\chi)
is an isomorphism of Von Neumann algebras.

For all
A\in\mathcal{A}
we define
\tilde{A}=(\sigma^{\chi}_{|\mu(\cons_{\LL}(\chi))})^{-1}(A)\in\mu(\cons_{\LL}(\chi))
. Consider
\ket{\phi_{00}}=\chi(\ket{00})
; by Hilbert-Schmidt decomposition it can be decomposed as
\ket{\phi_{00}}=\sum_{i}\lambda^{00}_{i}\ket{i_{\LL}}\ket{i_{\R}}
where
\{\ket{i_{\LL}}\}_{i\in I}
is an orthonormal family of
\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{\chi}
and
\{\ket{i_{\R}}\}_{i\in I}
is one of
\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{\chi}
. Similarly
\ket{\phi_{10}}=\chi(\ket{10})
can be decomposed as
\ket{\phi_{10}}=\sum_{j}\lambda^{10}_{j}\ket{j_{\LL}}\ket{j_{\R}}
. Let us now define
A=\outerproduct{0}{0}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\R}}
and
B=\outerproduct{1}{1}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\R}}
, which are two orthogonal and pairwise orthogonal projectors of
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
. Because
\sigma^{\chi}_{|\mu(\cons_{\LL}(\chi))}
is an isomorphism of Von Neumann algebras,
\tilde{A}
and
\tilde{B}
are also orthogonal and pairwise orthogonal projectors of
\cons_{\LL}(\chi)
such that
(\tilde{A}\otimes\mathbb{1})\ket{\phi_{00}}=\ket{\phi_{00}}
and
(\tilde{B}\otimes\mathbb{1})\ket{\phi_{10}}=\ket{\phi_{10}}
, i.e.
\ket{\phi_{00}}=\sum_{i}\lambda^{00}_{i}(\tilde{A}\ket{i_{\LL}})\ket{i_{\R}}
and
\ket{\phi_{10}}=\sum_{j}\lambda^{10}_{i}(\tilde{B}\ket{j_{\LL}})\ket{j_{\R}}
. Then because the
\{\ket{i_{\R}}\}
and
\{\ket{j_{\R}}\}
are families of orthonormal vectors, we obtain that for all
i
,
\ket{i_{\LL}}\in\imaginary(\tilde{A})
, and that for all
j
,
\ket{j_{\LL}}\in\imaginary(\tilde{B})
. As the two projectors are orthogonal we deduce that for all
i,j
,
\innerproduct{i_{\LL}}{j_{\LL}}=0
.

Now consider
C=\outerproduct{1}{0}\otimes\mathbb{1}\in\mathcal{A}
; its counterpart in
\mu(\cons_{\LL}(\chi))
,
\tilde{C}
, satisfies the same properties:
\tilde{C}^{\dagger}\tilde{C}=\tilde{A}
,
\tilde{C}\tilde{C}^{\dagger}=\tilde{B}
and
\tilde{C}\ket{\phi_{00}}=\ket{\phi_{10}}
. The two first properties imply that
\tilde{C}
acts unitarily between
\imaginary(\tilde{A})
and
\imaginary(\tilde{B})
. The third one tells us that
\ket{\phi_{10}}=\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}^{00}(\tilde{C}\ket{i_{\LL}})\ket{i_{\R}}
and then by unitarity that the
\tilde{C}\ket{i_{\LL}}
form an orthonormal family. It follows that we can rewrite it
\ket{\phi_{10}}=\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}^{00}\ket{(i+1)_{\LL}}\ket{i_{\R}}
. This reasoning generalizes to any choice of basis element on the left and on the right symmetrically, allowing us to write for all
l
and
r
,
\ket{\phi_{lr}}=\sum_{m}\lambda_{m}\ket{l+m}\ket{r+m}
.
∎

This proof can be understood as using the fact that
\chi:\mathcal{H}_{\LL}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\R}\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{\chi}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{\chi}
is a non-signalling perfect channel. The result of this lemma may seem a bit abstract but it can be stated more concretely when relating the shape of such splitting maps to canonical ones, as done in the next corollary.

Corollary 4.2
.

Let
\chi\in\balanced(\mathcal{H})
and suppose that
\mathcal{A}=\stloc_{\LL}(\chi)
is factor, then there exists
\zeta:\mathcal{H}\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}_{\LL}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\R}
a canonical splitting map for
\mathcal{A}
, a shared (entangled) state
\phi\in\mathcal{H}_{\MM}=\mathcal{H}_{{\MM}_{\LL}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{{\MM}_{\R}}
and isometries
U_{\LL}:\mathcal{H}_{\LL}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{{\MM}_{\LL}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{\chi}
and
U_{\R}:\mathcal{H}_{{\MM}_{\R}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\R}\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{\chi}
such that

\chi
=
\zeta
U_{L}
U_{R}
\phi

(30)

Proof.

Using Lemma
4.2
, the result is immediate by taking
\zeta
the canonical splitting map associated to the unitary
U:\ket{lr}\longrightarrow\ket{l}\otimes\ket{r}\in\mathcal{H}_{\LL}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\R}
(that appears in the proof),
\phi=\sum_{m}\lambda_{m}\ket{m}\ket{m}
,
U_{\LL}:\ket{l}\otimes\ket{m}\longrightarrow\ket{l+m}
and
U_{\R}:\ket{m}\otimes\ket{r}\longrightarrow\ket{r+m}
.
∎

Finally let us come back to general balanced splitting maps.

Corollary 4.3
.

Let
\chi\in\balanced(\mathcal{H})
and
\mathcal{A}=\stloc(\chi)
. Let
\{\pi_{i}\}
be the set of atomic projectors of
\mathcal{A}
, then there exists a family of canonical splitting maps for
\pi_{i}\mathcal{A}
,
\zeta_{i}:\pi_{i}\mathcal{H}\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}_{{\LL}_{i}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{{\R}_{i}}
, a family of entangled states
\phi_{i}\in\mathcal{H}_{{\MM}_{i}}=\mathcal{H}_{{\MM}_{{\LL}_{i}}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{{\MM}_{{\R}_{i}}}
and families of isometries
U_{{\LL}_{i}}:\mathcal{H}_{{\LL}_{i}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{{\MM}_{{\LL}_{i}}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{\chi}
and
U_{{\R}_{i}}:\mathcal{H}_{{\MM}_{{\R}_{i}}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{{\R}_{i}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{\chi}
such that

\chi
=
\zeta_{i}
U_{L_{i}}
U_{R_{i}}
\phi_{i}
\sum_{i}

(31)

Proof.

By Lemma
4.1
there exists a family of splitting maps
\chi_{i}
such that
\chi=\sum_{i}(\chi_{i})_{|\mathcal{H}_{i}}
. Because
\stloc_{\LL}(\chi)=\stloc_{\R}(\chi)^{\prime}
, both algebras share the same atomic projectors and thus the decomposition of
\chi
for the left algebra is also the decomposition of
\chi
for the right alegbra which implies that
\stloc_{\LL}((\chi_{i})_{|\mathcal{H}_{i}})=\pi_{i}\stloc_{\LL}(\chi)=\pi_{i}\stloc_{\R}(\chi)^{\prime}=\pi_{i}(\pi_{i}\stloc_{\R}(\chi))^{\prime}=\pi_{i}\stloc_{\R}((\chi_{i})_{|\mathcal{H}_{i}})^{\prime}=\stloc_{\R}((\chi_{i})_{|\mathcal{H}_{i}})^{\prime}
with
\stloc_{\LL}((\chi_{i})_{|\mathcal{H}_{i}})
and
\stloc_{\R}((\chi_{i})_{|\mathcal{H}_{i}})
factor. Then Lemma
4.2
gives us, for all
i
, the existence of the
\zeta_{i}
,
\phi_{i}
,
U_{{\LL}_{i}}
,
U_{{\R}_{i}}
and summing them concludes the proof.
∎

Remark that the balanced splitting maps have this property that we had witnessed with the examples
\chi_{\otimes}
and
\chi_{\oplus}
in section
3
: there is no difference between their local and strictly local operators. Indeed, let
\chi\in\balanced(\mathcal{H})
, we have that
\stloc_{\LL}(\chi)\subseteq\loc_{\LL}(\chi)\subseteq\stloc_{\R}(\chi)^{\prime}=\stloc_{\LL}(\chi)
with the inclusion being always true and the equality being the premise of the proposition. This proves that
\loc_{\LL}(\chi)=\stloc_{\LL}(\chi)
and a similar equality for the right local operators can be proved symmetrically.

Before proving the equivalence between balanced splitting maps and Von Neumann algebras, let us focus briefly on lean splitting maps and show that the algebraic requirement of being lean makes the shared states
\phi_{i}
in Corollary
4.3
separable; meaning that lean splitting maps are none other than canonical splitting maps followed by some local isometries.

Proposition 4.5
.

Let
\chi\in lean(\mathcal{H})
and
\mathcal{A}=\stloc(\chi)
. Then, there exists a canonical splitting map
\zeta
for
\mathcal{A}
and isometries
U_{\LL}
and
U_{\R}
such that
\chi=(U_{\LL}\otimes U_{\R})\zeta
, i.e.

\chi
=
\zeta
U_{L}
U_{R}

(32)

Proof.

Let
\chi\in\lean(\mathcal{H})
and
\mathcal{A}=\stloc(\chi)
. We know, by Lemma
4.1
, that we can decompose
\mathcal{H}=\bigoplus_{i}\mathcal{H}_{i}=\bigoplus\pi_{i}\mathcal{H}
and
\chi=\sum_{i}\chi_{i}=\sum_{i}\chi\pi_{i}
; and that there exist families
\{\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{i}\}
of pairwise orthogonal subspaces of
\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{\chi}
, and
\{\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{i}\}
of pairwise orthogonal subspaces of
\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{\chi}
such that
\imaginary(\chi_{i})\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{i}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{i}
. We also know that, for all
i
,
\ker(\chi_{i})=(\mathcal{H}_{i})^{\perp}
and that, by Lemma
4.2
,
(\chi_{i})_{|\mathcal{H}_{i}}:\ket{lr}=\sum_{m}\lambda_{m}\ket{l+m}\ket{r+m}\in\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{i}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{i}
. Remark that, because
\{\mathcal{H}_{i}\}
,
\{\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{i}\}_{i}
and
\{\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{i}\}_{i}
are all families of pairwise orthogonal subspaces, commuting with
\chi\chi^{\dagger}
is precisely equivalent to commuting with each of the
\chi_{i}\chi_{i}^{\dagger}
. It follows that
\cons_{\LL}(\chi)=\bigcap_{i}\cons_{\LL}((\chi_{i})_{|\mathcal{H}_{i}})
. Let
A\in\cons_{\LL}((\chi_{i})_{|\mathcal{H}_{i}})
, which we know by Lemma
3.1
to be the algebra of operators
A\in\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{\chi}
such that
\imaginary(\chi_{i})
and
\imaginary(\chi_{i})^{\perp}
are invariant subspaces for
A\otimes\mathbb{1}
. One can check that
A
is of the form
U_{{\LL}_{i}}(\tilde{A}\otimes\mathbb{1})U_{{\LL}_{i}}^{\dagger}\oplus\tilde{B}
where
U_{{\LL}_{i}}
is the isometry (defined in Corollary
4.3
) such that
U_{{\LL}_{i}}:\ket{l}\ket{m}\rightarrow\ket{l+m}
and
\tilde{B}
an operator in
\mathcal{L}(\Span(\{\ket{l+m}\})^{\perp})
. Conversely, one can also check that any
A
of this form is
\chi_{i}
-consistent.

Let us now suppose that there exists
i
such that
\phi_{i}
(also defined in Corollary
4.3
) is not separable, or equivalently such that the corresponding decomposition
(\chi_{i})_{|\mathcal{H}_{i}}:\ket{lr}=\sum_{m}\lambda_{m}\ket{l+m}\ket{r+m}
has more than one term. Let us call
m_{0}
and
m_{1}
the two first indices and consider the non-trivial map
C=U_{{\LL}_{i}}(\mathbb{1}\otimes\outerproduct{m_0}{m_1})U_{{\LL}_{i}}^{\dagger}
. This operator commutes by construction with every
\chi_{i}
-consistent operator. It follows that
C\in\cons_{\LL}((\chi_{i})_{|\mathcal{H}_{i}})^{\prime}\subseteq\cons_{\LL}(\chi)^{\prime}
. However
C\notin\cons_{\LL}((\chi_{i})_{|\mathcal{H}_{i}})
and thus
C\notin\cons_{\LL}(\chi)
, which contradicts the assumption of
\chi
being lean. It follows that for all
i
,
\phi_{i}
is separable or equivalently that
(\chi_{i})_{|\mathcal{H}_{i}}:\ket{lr}\rightarrow\ket{l+0}\ket{r+0}
. Defining
\zeta:\ket{lr}\rightarrow\ket{l}\otimes\ket{r}
,
U_{\LL}:\ket{l}\rightarrow\ket{l+0}
and
U_{\R}:\ket{r}\rightarrow\ket{r+0}
provides the desired decomposition.
∎

We will now express our equivalence theorem between subalgebras of
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
and their inclusions, and splitting maps on
\mathcal{H}
and their comprehension.

Theorem 4.1
.

The class of balanced splitting maps is the biggest class of splitting maps making
F/G
an equivalence of preorders.

Proof.

First we remind that
F
and
G
being preorder preserving is respectively the result of Proposition
4.2
and Proposition
4.4
.

Let us now prove that these two maps yield an equivalence of preorders. Let
\mathcal{A}\in\vnalg(\mathcal{H})
,
F\circ G(\mathcal{A})=\stloc(\chi)
with
\chi
a canonical splitting map for
\mathcal{A}
. It immediately follows that
F\circ G(\mathcal{A})=\mathcal{A}
and thus that
F\circ G(\mathcal{A})\subseteq\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{A}\subseteq F\circ G(\mathcal{A})
.

Conversely, let
\chi
be a balanced splitting map. We then have that
G\circ F(\chi)=G(\stloc(\chi))=\zeta
is a canonical splitting map for
\mathcal{A}=\stloc(\chi)
and we want to prove that
\zeta\sqsubseteq\chi
and
\chi\sqsubseteq\zeta
. First, let us remark that it is sufficient to prove the result in the case where
\mathcal{A}
is factor. Indeed, by Lemma
4.1
we know that the
\chi_{i}=\chi\pi_{i}
are such that
\imaginary(\chi_{i})\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{i}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{i}
where the
\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{i}
are pairwise orthogonal subspaces of
\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{\chi}
and the
\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{i}
pairwise orthogonal subspaces of
\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{\chi}
. In the same way, we can obtain a decomposition of
\zeta
, as the sum of the
\zeta_{i}=\zeta\pi_{i}
whose images have similar orthogonality properties as the ones of the
\chi_{i}
. Remark then that, for all
i
, the
(\chi_{i})_{|\mathcal{H}_{i}=\pi_{i}\mathcal{H}}
and
(\zeta_{i})_{|\mathcal{H}_{i}}
are respectively balanced and canonical splitting maps for the factor algebra
\mathcal{A}_{i}=\pi_{i}\mathcal{A}
. Suppose now that, for all
i
, we have
\chi_{i}\sqsubseteq\zeta_{i}
, i.e. we have isometries
\bigcirc_{i}
and
\hbox to8.4pt{\vbox to8.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.2pt\lower-4.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}

such that
(\bigcirc_{i}\otimes\mathbb{1})\chi_{i}=(\mathbb{1}\otimes\hbox to8.4pt{\vbox to8.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.2pt\lower-4.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}
