. Because the
\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{i}
such that
\imaginary(\chi_{i})\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{i}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{i}
are orthogonal (and because the same can be said for the lefty branch of the
\zeta_{i}
), choosing
\bigcirc=\oplus(\bigcirc_{i})_{|\mathcal{H}_{i}}
and similarly for
, we obtain that
(\bigcirc\otimes\mathbb{1})\chi=(\mathbb{1}\otimes\hbox to8.4pt{\vbox to8.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.2pt\lower-4.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 5.53 0 C 5.53 3.06 3.06 5.53 0 5.53 C -3.06 5.53 -5.53 3.06 -5.53 0 C -5.53 -3.06 -3.06 -5.53 0 -5.53 C 3.06 -5.53 5.53 -3.06 5.53 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}})\zeta
proving that
\chi\sqsubseteq\zeta
. A symmetric reasoning proves the implication for the reverse comprehension. It remains to show that, for all
i
,
\chi_{i}\sqsubseteq\zeta_{i}
and
\zeta_{i}\sqsubseteq\chi_{i}
, i.e. to prove the result in the factor case.

Suppose thus that
\mathcal{A}
is factor. By Lemma
4.2
, there exists a basis
\{\ket{lr}\}
orthonormal families
\{\ket{l+m}\}
and
\{\ket{r+m}\}
such that
\chi(\ket{lr})=\sum_{m}\lambda_{m}\ket{l+m}\ket{r+m}
. By construction of
\{\ket{lr}\}
, and because
\zeta
is a canonical splitting map associated to the same algebra
\mathcal{A}
, we have that
\zeta(\ket{lr})=\ket{\phi_l}\otimes\ket{\psi_r}
. Let’s define
\bigcirc:\ket{l+m}\longrightarrow\ket{\phi_l}\otimes\ket{m}
and
\hbox to8.4pt{\vbox to8.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.2pt\lower-4.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 5.53 0 C 5.53 3.06 3.06 5.53 0 5.53 C -3.06 5.53 -5.53 3.06 -5.53 0 C -5.53 -3.06 -3.06 -5.53 0 -5.53 C 3.06 -5.53 5.53 -3.06 5.53 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}:\ket{\phi_r}\longrightarrow\sum_{m}\lambda_{m}\ket{m}\otimes\ket{m+r}
, we get that
(\bigcirc\otimes\mathbb{1})\chi(\ket{lr})=\sum_{m}\lambda_{m}\ket{\phi_l}\otimes\ket{m}\otimes\ket{m+r}=\ket{\phi_l}\otimes(\sum_{m}\lambda_{m}\ket{m}\otimes\ket{m+r})=(\mathbb{1}\otimes\hbox to8.4pt{\vbox to8.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.2pt\lower-4.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 5.53 0 C 5.53 3.06 3.06 5.53 0 5.53 C -3.06 5.53 -5.53 3.06 -5.53 0 C -5.53 -3.06 -3.06 -5.53 0 -5.53 C 3.06 -5.53 5.53 -3.06 5.53 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}})\zeta(\ket{lr})
. A symmetric reasoning shows that
\chi\sqsubseteq\zeta
, proving that
F/G
is then an equivalence. The fact that is the biggest class making it an equivalence is then the result of Remark
4.2
.
∎

As a result, we have established a basic equivalence (as preorders) between the inclusion of Von Neumann algebras and the comprehension of balanced splitting maps,

(\vnalg(\mathcal{H}),\sqsubseteq)\cong(\balanced(\mathcal{H}),\subseteq).

(33)

After showing how to relate the algebraic view on quantum subsystems with our diagrammatic view, we have proved that the latter was able to fully capture the former by looking at the subclass of balanced splitting maps that form an equivalent theory to the one of general algebraic quantum subsystems. We will now make use of the diagrammatic approach to develop an understanding of the relationship between causality and localisability for general quantum subsystems.

5
Trace and non-signalling

In the previous sections, we defined splitting maps in the context of pure quantum theory, as isometries splitting the space of states. In this section we will extend, in a very natural way, the notion of splitting map to isometric channels acting on density matrices. We will then define a notion of trace over a splitting map and show how it can be related to definition
2.4
of the trace over a Von Neumann algebra. We will then use these tools to prove a new result of causal decompositions of quantum channels that are non-signalling from an algebra to another.

5.1
Mixed splitting maps and
\chi
-trace

In the previous sections of this article, we had taken the point of view of pure quantum theory: the global system that we were trying to split was described by the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
and was given some additional structure through the splitting map. The question of extending splitting maps to acting on mixed quantum states follows very naturally and it can actually be answered quite simply, by doubling the isometry, just as it would be done for a unitary that we would like to see as a channel
[
20
]
.

Definition 5.1

(Mixed splitting map)
.

Given a pure splitting map
\chi:\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{\chi}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{\chi}
, we define the associated mixed splitting map
\chi:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})\rightarrow\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{\chi}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{\chi})
as
\chi(\rho)=\chi\rho\chi^{\dagger}
. Diagrammatically, we will write it as

\rho
\chi
:=
\rho
\chi
\chi

(34)

where the right part is an unfolding in pure quantum theory diagrams of the left, which is a mixed theory diagram.

The natural follow-up is the question of the partial trace over a splitting map. The side of the splitting over which we will trace has to be chosen as convention; we decide to call trace over
\chi
, the trace over the right side of
\chi
, matching the definition of
[
5
]
.

Definition 5.2

(
\chi
-trace)
.

Let
\chi:\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{\chi}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{\chi}
. The partial trace over
\chi
, which we will call
\chi
-trace, is the map
\Tr_{\chi}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})\rightarrow\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{\chi})
defined by
\Tr_{\chi}(\cdot)=\Tr_{\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{\chi}}(\chi\cdot\chi^{\dagger})
. Diagrammatically we will write it

(35)

In particular, when
\chi
is a lean splitting map, we obtain that the
\chi
-trace is equal (up to an isometry due to the choice of representation) to the trace (as it is defined in Definition
2.4
) over the algebra
\stloc_{\R}(\chi)
; this strengthens the idea that splitting maps can capture perfectly the theory of quantum systems as Von Neumann algebras.

Theorem 5.1
.

Let
\mathcal{B}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
be a Von Neumann algebra and let
\chi:\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{\chi}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{\chi}
be a lean splitting map such that
\stloc_{\R}(\chi)=\mathcal{B}
. Then there exists an isometry
U
such that
\Tr_{\chi}(\cdot)=U\Tr_{\mathcal{B}}(\cdot)U^{\dagger}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{B}\in\vnalg(\mathcal{H})
. First, let us prove this result when
\chi
is canonical. By Theorem
2.2
, there exists a representation unitary
V:\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\bigoplus_{i}(\mathcal{H}_{{\LL}}^{i}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{{\R}}^{i})
for the pair of algebras
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
on the left and
\mathcal{B}
on the right. In the rest of this proof, we will identify
\mathcal{H}
and
\bigoplus_{i}(\mathcal{H}_{{\LL}}^{i}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{{\R}}^{i})
, as it is only a matter of defining some structure on
\mathcal{H}
relative to the algebras
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{B}
. The trace over
\mathcal{B}
is then defined as
\Tr_{\mathcal{B}}(\cdot)=\bigoplus_{i}\Tr_{\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{i}}(\pi_{i}\cdot\pi_{i})
, where the
\pi_{i}
are the projectors on the
\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{i}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{i}
, i.e. the atomic projectors of
\mathcal{B}
.

Let now
\chi
be a canonical splitting map such that
\stloc_{\R}(\chi)=\mathcal{B}
. By definition there exists another representation unitary
\tilde{V}:\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\bigoplus_{i}(\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{{\LL}}^{i}\otimes\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{{\R}}^{i})
, such that
\chi
is simply the embedding
\mathcal{H}\cong\bigoplus_{i}(\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{{\LL}}^{i}\otimes\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{{\R}}^{i})\hookrightarrow(\oplus_{i}\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{{\LL}}^{i})\otimes(\oplus_{i}\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{{\R}}^{i})
. We will call
\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{\LL}=\oplus_{i}\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{{\LL}}^{i}
,
\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{\R}=\oplus_{i}\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{{\R}}^{i}
and
\tilde{\pi}_{i}
the respective projectors on the
\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{\LL}^{i}\otimes\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{\R}^{i}
. Because
\tilde{V}
is also a representation unitary for the pair of algebras
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
on the left and
\mathcal{B}
on the right, it is of the form
\tilde{V}=\bigoplus_{i}(U_{\LL}^{i}\otimes U_{\R}^{i})
where the
U_{\LL}^{i}:\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{i}\rightarrow\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{\LL}^{i}
are unitaries (and similarly on the right for the
U_{\R}^{i}
). We then have that, for all
\rho\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
,
\Tr_{\chi}(\rho)=\Tr_{\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{\R}}(\chi\rho\chi^{\dagger})=\sum_{i,j}\Tr_{\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{\R}}(\chi\pi_{i}\rho\pi_{j}\chi^{\dagger})=\sum_{i,j,k}\Tr_{\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{\R}^{k}}(\chi\pi_{i}\rho\pi_{j}\chi^{\dagger})=\sum_{i,j,k}\Tr_{\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{\R}^{k}}((U_{\LL}^{i}\otimes U_{\R}^{i})\pi_{i}\rho\pi_{j}(U_{\LL}^{j}\otimes U_{\R}^{j})^{\dagger})=\sum_{i}\Tr_{\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{\R}^{i}}((U_{\LL}^{i}\otimes U_{\R}^{i})\pi_{i}\rho\pi_{i}(U_{\LL}^{i}\otimes U_{\R}^{i})^{\dagger})=\sum_{i}\Tr_{\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{i}}((U_{\LL}^{i}\otimes\mathbb{1})\pi_{i}\rho\pi_{i}(U_{\LL}^{i}\otimes\mathbb{1})^{\dagger})=U\Tr(\rho)U^{\dagger}
where
U=\bigoplus_{i}U_{\LL}^{i}
.

Let us now go back to the general case and suppose that
\chi
is any lean splitting map. Let
\mathcal{A}=\stloc(\chi)
and
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{A}^{\prime}=\stloc_{\R}(\chi)
. By Proposition
4.5
, we know that there exist a canonical splitting map for
\mathcal{A}
and isometries
W_{\LL}
and
W_{\R}
such that
\chi=(W_{\LL}\otimes W_{\R})\zeta
. It follows that
\Tr_{\chi}(\cdot)=\Tr_{\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{\chi}}(\chi\cdot\chi^{\dagger})=\Tr_{\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{\chi}}((W_{\LL}\otimes W_{\R})\zeta\cdot\zeta^{\dagger}(W_{\LL}^{\dagger}\otimes W_{\R}^{\dagger}))=\Tr_{\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{\zeta}}((W_{\LL}\otimes\mathbb{1})\zeta\cdot\zeta^{\dagger}(W_{\LL}^{\dagger}\otimes\mathbb{1}))=W_{\LL}\Tr_{\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{\zeta}}(\zeta\cdot\zeta^{\dagger})W_{\LL}^{\dagger}=W_{\LL}U\Tr_{\mathcal{B}}(\cdot)U^{\dagger}W_{\LL}^{\dagger}
, where the last equality comes from applying this theorem that we’ve just proved to be true in the canonical case to
\zeta
.
∎

Let us quickly remark that this theorem is not true in the case of general balanced splitting maps but only in the case of the lean ones. The idea is that if one of the
\phi_{i}
is not separable in the decomposition of
\chi
given by Corollary
4.2
, then the
\chi
-trace, by tracing the right part of
\phi_{i}
, will induce some decoherence compared to the Von Neumann algebra trace.

As we want to focus, in this last section, on signalling between algebras and causal decompositions, we will only consider splitting maps that behave like Von Neumann algebras for their inclusion but also for their trace, i.e. lean splitting maps. Let us now express the notions of semi-causality and semi-locality in terms of splitting maps.

Definition 5.3

(Heisenberg semi-causality)
.

We say that a unitary
U:\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}
is Heisenberg semi-causal from
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
to
\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{K})
if

[U^{\dagger}\mathcal{B}U,\mathcal{A}]=0\,.

(36)

In terms of splitting maps, this is equivalent to asking that for any choice of lean splitting maps
\chi_{\mathcal{A}}
,
\chi_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}
and
\chi_{\mathcal{B}}
representing the corresponding algebras and for any
B\in\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{\chi_{\mathcal{B}}}
, there exist
A_{1}\in\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{\chi_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}}
and
A_{2}\in\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{\chi_{\mathcal{A}}}
such that

(37)

Definition 5.4

(Schrödinger semi-causality)
.

We say that a channel
\mathcal{E}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})\rightarrow\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{K})
is Schrödinger semi-causal from
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
to
\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{K})
if there exists a completely positive and trace-preserving (CPTP) map
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
such that

\Tr_{\mathcal{B^{\prime}}}[\mathcal{E}(\cdot)]=\mathcal{E}^{\prime}(\Tr_{\mathcal{A}}[\cdot])\,.

(38)

This condition can equivalently be phrased as the fact that for any choice of lean splitting maps
\chi_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}
and
\chi_{\mathcal{B}}
there exists a CPTP map
\tilde{\mathcal{E}}
such that

(39)

In the particular case where
\mathcal{E}
is a unitary channel, i.e. of the form
\mathcal{E}(\cdot)=U\cdot U^{\dagger}
with
U:\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}
unitary, we obtain the following result:

Theorem 5.2
.

A unitary is Heisenberg semi-causal from
\mathcal{A}
to
\mathcal{B}
if and only if it is Schrödinger semi-causal from
\mathcal{A}
to
\mathcal{B}
.

Proof.

Let
U:\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}
be a unitary and let
\chi_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}
and
\chi_{\mathcal{B}}
be lean splitting maps representing the corresponding algebras. Suppose that for all
B
, there exists
A
such that

{\hbox to182.08pt{\vbox to157.49pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 50.25554pt\lower-78.74509pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \hbox to0.0pt{
{
{}{}{}}{}

(40)

It follows that
\Tr_{\chi_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}}(\rho)=\Tr_{\chi_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}}(\sigma)\implies\Tr_{\chi_{\mathcal{B}}}(U\rho U^{\dagger})=\Tr_{\chi_{\mathcal{B}}}(U\sigma U^{\dagger})
. Indeed, let
\rho
and
\sigma
satisfying the aforementioned premise; then for all

(41)

and by removing the map and bending the wires back (which we can do because the equality is true for all
B
), we have proved that
\Tr_{\chi_{\mathcal{B}}}(U\rho U^{\dagger})=\Tr_{\chi_{\mathcal{B}}}(U\sigma U^{\dagger})
.

Recall now that, as
\chi_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}
is lean, its image is of the form
\imaginary(\chi_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}})=\bigoplus_{i}\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{i}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{i}\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{\chi_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{\chi_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}}
. Let us construct the isometry
V:\bigoplus_{i}\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{i}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
such that, for all
i
,
V_{|\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{i}}\ket{\psi^{i}_{\LL}}:=\chi_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}^{\dagger}(\ket{\psi^{i}_{{\LL}}}\otimes\ket{0^{i}_{\R}})
with
\ket{0^{i}_{\R}}\in\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{i}
. Let
\{\ket{k}\}_{k}
be an orthonormal basis of
(\bigoplus_{i}\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{i})^{\perp}
and choose some
\ket{\phi}\in\mathcal{H}
. We define for all
k
a map
V_{k}=\outerproduct{\phi}{k}
. It follows that
\mathcal{F}=V\cdot V^{\dagger}+\sum_{k}V_{k}\cdot V_{k}^{\dagger}
is a quantum channel from
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{\chi_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}})
to
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
that has the property that
\Tr_{\chi_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}}(\mathcal{F}(\Tr_{\chi_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}}(\rho)))=\Tr_{\chi_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}}(\rho)
. We finally define the channel
\tilde{\mathcal{E}}:\rho\rightarrow\Tr_{\chi_{\mathcal{B}}}(U\mathcal{F}(\rho)U^{\dagger})
. We can compute that
\tilde{\mathcal{E}}(\Tr_{\chi_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}}(\rho))=\Tr_{\chi_{\mathcal{B}}}(U\mathcal{F}(\Tr_{\chi_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}}(\rho))U^{\dagger})=\Tr_{\chi_{\mathcal{B}}}(U\rho U^{\dagger})
where the last equality comes from the fact that
\rho
and
\mathcal{F}(\Tr_{\chi_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}}(\rho))
have the same
\chi_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}
-trace and thus the same
\chi_{\mathcal{B}}
-trace after applying
U\cdot U^{\dagger}
.

Conversely, suppose that
U
is Schrödinger semi-causal. Then for all
\rho
,

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{56.90552pt}{-49.79233pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\hss}\lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\,.

(43)

As these implications are true for any choice of
\chi_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}
and
\chi_{\mathcal{B}}
, we have proved that
U
is Heisenberg semi-causal if and only if it is Schrödinger semi-causal.
∎

This shows that the tracing-style definition, or equivalently the discarding-style definition, is not in some way incomplete and that it captures precisely the usual coherent notion of non-signalling in terms of commutation of operators when restricted to unitaries.

Let us now remark that the Schrödinger causality, in its splitting maps phrasing, plays naturally with the notion of comprehension. Indeed, suppose that
\mathcal{E}
is semi-causal from
\mathcal{A}
to
\mathcal{B}
and let
\chi\sqsubseteq\chi_{B}
; then

(45)

Remark that this implies that, to prove Schrödinger semi-causality, it is sufficient to prove equality (
39
) for one pair of corresponding lean splitting maps, as the mutual comprehension of lean splitting maps representing the same algebra will imply that the equality is true for any such pair. This computation also proves that even if the
\chi
-trace and the algebraic trace agree only in the case of lean splitting maps, semi-causality can actually be stated equivalently for any pair of balanced splitting maps respectively representing
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
.
Let us now give the definition of semi-localisability between a pair of splitting maps. We state it here in full generality but we will actually only use it in the rest of this section in the case of lean splitting maps.

Definition 5.5

(Semi-localisability)
.

A channel
\mathcal{E}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})\longrightarrow\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{K})
is semi-localisable from
\chi\in\spl(\mathcal{H})
to
\zeta\in\spl(\mathcal{K})
if and only if there exist channels
\mathcal{E}_{1}
and
\mathcal{E}_{2}
such that it can be decomposed as

(48)

Theorem 5.3
.

A channel
\mathcal{E}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})\longrightarrow\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{K})
is semi-causal from
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
to
\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{K})
if and only if there exist
\chi_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}\in\lean(\mathcal{H})
and
\chi_{\mathcal{B}}\in\lean(\mathcal{K})
, respectively representing
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{B}
, such that
\mathcal{E}
is semi-localisable from
\chi_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}
to
\chi_{\mathcal{B}}
.

Proof.

Suppose that the channel
\mathcal{E}
it is semi-localisable from
\chi_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}
to
\chi_{\mathcal{B}}
. Then immediately

(51)

Let us consider
\zeta_{\mathcal{B}}=(\mathbb{1}\otimes W)\chi_{\mathcal{B}}
where
W:\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{\chi_{\mathcal{B}}}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{\zeta_{\mathcal{B}}}
is an isometry such that
\dim(\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{\zeta_{\mathcal{B}}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{U})\geq\dim(\mathcal{H}_{V}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{\chi_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}})
. Remark that, by construction,
\zeta_{\mathcal{B}}
is also a lean splitting map representing the algebra
\mathcal{B}
. We have that

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\,.

(52)

In particular this implies that

\zeta_{\mathcal{B}}
U
\chi_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}
V
and
\mathcal{H}_{L}^{\zeta_{\mathcal{B}}}
\mathcal{H}_{R}^{\zeta_{\mathcal{B}}}
\mathcal{H}_{U}
\mathcal{H}_{L}^{\zeta_{\mathcal{B}}}
\mathcal{H}_{V}
\mathcal{H}_{R}^{\chi_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}}

(53)

are two dilations of the same map. Because
\dim(\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{\zeta_{\mathcal{B}}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{U})\geq\dim(\mathcal{H}_{V}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{\chi_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}})
, Lemma
D.1
tells us that there exists an isometry
T
such that

\zeta_{\mathcal{B}}
U
\chi_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}
V
=
T

(54)

Then, because
U
is a Stinespring dilation of
\mathcal{E}
,

\zeta_{\mathcal{B}}
U
\chi_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}

(57)

∎

Remark 5.1
.

The fact that the semi-causality of a channel
\mathcal{E}
is not equivalent to its semi-localisability with respect to any pair of lean splitting maps representing the algebras is only a problem of the dimensions of the Hilbert spaces involved in the representations not being compatible. Indeed when we construct
\zeta_{\mathcal{B}}
, we have to ensure that
\dim(\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{\zeta_{\mathcal{B}}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{U})\geq\dim(\mathcal{H}_{V}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{\chi_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}})
so that
T
is an isometry from
\mathcal{H}_{V}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{\chi_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}}
to
\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{\zeta_{\mathcal{B}}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{U}
and not the other way around.

This is a causally faithful decomposition theorem for arbitrary finite-dimensional quantum subsystems. It shows both how splitting maps can be used to prove structural theorems regarding causality and locality, and even give a neat way to frame them to begin with.

6
Conclusion

In this article, we have defined the notion of splitting map as a tool to decompose a system into a pair of disjoint subsystems. We have shown that we were able to define some notion of (strict) locality with respect to a splitting map, through which we could link our diagrammatic approach to general algebraic quantum subsystems. We have equipped the set of splitting maps on a system with a preorder named comprehension and have then defined a few particular classes of splitting maps. The one of canonical splitting maps, given by the Artin-Wedderburn theorem, that are constructive representations of algebraic subsystems. The first one is that of balanced splitting maps which we showed to be the biggest class that is equivalent (as a preordered set) to the theory of algebraic quantum subsystems; and the second is its subclass of lean splitting maps, that are able to diagrammatically capture the trace over the algebra they represent. Finally, we used this feature to obtain a causal decomposition for channels that are non-signalling from an algebra to another, generalising the result that was given in
[
24
]
in the factor case.

A natural follow-up is the question of how precisely this approach relates to that of
[
18
,
3
,
39
]
, which develops a compositional representation for non-factor subsystems using additional features and known graphical representations for
2
-categories. For instance, whilst there is a natural way to imagine constructing
2
-categories analogous to the
2
-category of
2
-Hilbert spaces
[
7
]
from bimonoidal categories which add an additional monoidal structure analogous to the direct sum
[
32
]
, the approach outlined in this paper suggests there could be a construction which achieves the same, but which simply leverages the basic notion of splitting internal to a monoidal category. In other words, we wonder if it could be possible to consider the identification of splitting maps and comprehension within monoidal categories as a new way to split an entire dagger monoidal category into a
2
-category.

The results presented in this paper appear to be a satisfactory approach to identifying non-factor subsystems within the language of symmetric monoidal categories, but possibly not a satisfactory diagrammatic approach. While splitting, locality, trace, and comprehension are simple interpretable diagrammatic equations or definitions, the same cannot be said for the representation that we have given for maps that are balanced or lean. In future work we intend to give a completely equational diagrammatic representation of those features too, in order to take a step closure to a fully diagrammatic identification of general quantum subsystems.

Finally, the traditional way to think of non-factor subsystems is in terms of decomposition; however, the dagger of the splitting map appears to represent an internal compositional representation in terms of a generalised tensor product of the kind explored in
[
5
,
4
]
. For future work, it therefore seems promising to explore whether the established properties of splitting maps, in particular comprehension which mimics the associativity of composition for factor systems, really allow for a constructive/compositional way to reason with non-factor subsystems.

Appendix A
Algebra results

We remind a result on the shape of Von Neumann algebra homomorphisms
[
46
]
.

Proposition A.1
.

Let
f:\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}})\to\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}})
be a homomorphism of Von Neumann algebras and let
\Atproj(\mathcal{A})=\{\pi_{i}\}_{i\in I}
. Let us define
I_{f}:=\{i\in I|f(\pi_{i})\neq 0\}
, then the orthogonal projectors
\mu:=\sum_{i\in I_{f}}\pi_{i}
and
{\bar{\mu}}:=\mathbb{1}-\mu=\sum_{i\in I\setminus I_{f}}\pi^{i}
are such that:

\mathcal{A}=\mu\mathcal{A}\oplus{\bar{\mu}}\mathcal{A}\,;

(58a)

\ker f={\bar{\mu}}\mathcal{A}\,;

(58b)

f|_{\mu\mathcal{A}}\textrm{ is injective.}

(58c)

Proof.

The proof of
58a
is immediate by writing the identity operator as the sum of the
\pi_{i}
and splitting it into two parts, for
\mu
and
{\bar{\mu}}
respectively. By definition of
I_{f}
and
\mu
, the reverse inclusion of
58b
is also immediate. Let us now prove that
\ker f\subseteq{\bar{\mu}}\mathcal{A}
.

Suppose that it is not the case and that there exists an
A\in\ker f\setminus{\bar{\mu}}\mathcal{A}
. Because
{\bar{\mu}}\mathcal{A}\subseteq\ker f
, we have that
f(\mu A)=f(\mu A)+f({\bar{\mu}}A)=f(A)=0
and thus without loss of generality we can suppose that
0\neq A\in\mu\mathcal{A}\cap\ker f
.

It follows that
AA^{\dagger}
is a non-zero positive element of
\mu\mathcal{A}
which is isomorphic to a
\bigoplus_{i\in I_{f}}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}}^{i})
by theorem
2.3
. By spectral theorem
AA^{\dagger}
can be decomposed as
AA^{\dagger}=\sum_{k}\alpha_{k}\nu_{k}
where the
\nu_{k}
are non-zero orthogonal and pairwise orthogonal rank-
1
projectors of
\mu\mathcal{A}
and the
\alpha_{k}
are positive real numbers. Because
f
is an homomorphism of Von Neumann algebras, the
f(\nu_{k})
inherit the properties of the
\nu_{k}
and are also a family of orthogonal and pairwise orthogonal rank-
1
projectors. We can then compute that

0=f(A)f(A^{\dagger})=f(AA^{\dagger})=f(\sum_{k}\alpha_{k}\nu_{k})=\sum_{k}\alpha_{k}f(\nu_{k})

(59)

which implies that for all
k
, if
\alpha_{k}\neq 0
, then
f(\nu_{k})=0
. And because
A\neq 0
, at least one of the
f(\nu_{k})
is null. Without loss of generality let us suppose that it is
f(\nu_{0})
. As
\nu_{0}\in\mu\mathcal{A}
,
\nu_{0}=\mu\nu_{0}=\sum_{i\in I_{f}}\pi_{i}\nu_{0}
and because
\nu_{0}
is of rank
1
there exists a unique
i\in I_{f}
such that
\nu_{0}\in\pi_{i}\mathcal{A}
.

Identifying
\pi_{i}\mathcal{A}
with its representation
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}}^{i})
given by theorem
2.3
, we can chose an orthonormal basis
\{\ket{j}\}
of
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}}^{i}
such that
\nu_{0}=\outerproduct{0}{0}
. Remark that
\pi_{i}\mathcal{A}
is then spanned by the set
\{\outerproduct{m}{n}\}
but also that

f(\outerproduct{m}{n})=f(\outerproduct{m}{0})f(\outerproduct{0}{0})f(\outerproduct{0}{n})=f(\outerproduct{m}{0})0f(\outerproduct{0}{n})=0

(60)

It follows that
\pi_{i}\mathcal{A}\subseteq\ker h
and thus that
\pi_{i}\in\ker h
, which is not possible as
i\in I_{f}
. This proves that
\ker f\setminus{\bar{\mu}}\mathcal{A}=\emptyset
and that
\ker f={\bar{\mu}}\mathcal{A}
. It also follows that
\ker f|_{\mu\mathcal{A}}=\mu\mathcal{A}\cap\ker f=\mu\mathcal{A}\cap{\bar{\mu}}\mathcal{A}=\{0\}
proving
58c
.
∎

Appendix B
Preorder preservation of the AW representation

We now give the proof of the following proposition, stating that any choice of a canonical splitting map for every
\mathcal{A}\in\vnalg(\mathcal{H})
will be a preorder preserving map from
\vnalg(\mathcal{H})
to
\spl(\mathcal{H})
.

Proposition B.1
.

Let
\mathcal{A}_{S}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{B}
be two Von Neumann subalgebras of
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
. Let
\chi:\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{B}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{B}
and
\zeta:\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{S}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{S}
be canonical splitting maps for
\mathcal{A}_{B}
and
\mathcal{A}_{S}
respectively. Then
\zeta\sqsubseteq\chi
.

Proof.

The proof of this proposition is based on the constructive proof of Artin-Wedderburn theorem (in the case of finite dimensional Von Neumann algebras) given in
[
36
]
. We will first explain, without going into full details, how these representation unitaries can be constructed and then prove the comprehension of the splitting maps.

Let
P=\{P_{i}\}_{i=1\ldots p}
be a family of non-zero orthogonal and 2-by-2 orthogonal projectors of
\mathcal{A}
, i.e.

(i)

\forall i=1\ldots p\;0\neq P_{i}=P_{i}^{\dagger}\in\mathcal{A}
;

(ii)

\forall i,j=1\ldots p\;P_{i}P_{j}=\delta_{ij}P_{i}\in\mathcal{A}
.

Notice that such a family always exists as
\{\mathbb{1}\}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
is one.

Moreover we will take
P
to be a maximal among such families, i.e. so that there is no set
Q=\{Q_{i}\}
verifying conditions
(i),(ii)
and such that
\mathcal{P}\subset\mathcal{Q}
, with

\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q}
the algebraic closures of
P,Q
. As the
P_{i}
are orthogonal projectors, we can understand maximality as saying that no
P_{i}
can be decomposed as the sum of orthogonal projectors of
\mathcal{A}
that would be orthogonal one to the other and to all the other
P_{j}
.
Finally note that:

(iii)

\sum_{i=1\ldots p}P_{i}=\mathbb{1}
,

otherwise
\mathbb{1}-\sum_{i=1\ldots p}P_{i}
may be added to the set.

Such a family has the following properties:

•

The following relation
P_{i}RP_{j}\Leftrightarrow P_{i}\mathcal{A}P_{j}\neq 0
is an equivalence relation. Projectors of the same class
I
are in the same block
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{I})\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{I}}
of the Artin-Wedderburn decomposition. Different blocks are in direct sum.

•

For all
i
,
P_{i}\mathcal{A}P_{i}=\mathbb{C}P_{i}
. This comes from the fact that the family is maximal. The
P_{i}
indicate redudancies that will be seen as tensoring with the identity in a block
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{I})\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{I}}
of the decomposition.

•

Projectors of the same class have the same rank

Because the
P_{i}
of a same class
I
form an orthogonal, complete set of projectors of equal dimension it is possible to find a unitary
U_{I}
that respectively sends them to
\outerproduct{i}{i}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{I}}
(where
\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{I}
is of dimension
q
their common rank). This
U_{I}
is the data of a mapping from
\imaginary(P_{i})
into
\mathcal{H}_{{\R}_{I}}
for for each
i\in I
but nothing forces yet the different mappings to match with respect to what can be done in
\mathcal{A}
. This is why in the end the unitary for a block (or equivalence class) is the following

W_{I}=\sum_{i}(\outerproduct{i}{1_I}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{I}})UP_{1_{I}}AP_{i}

(61)

and the general mapping is the sum of these mapping for each blocks. One can easily show that any representation unitary
W
is of this form. It suffices to take
P_{i}
as the preimage of
\outerproduct{i}{i}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{I}}
and U as the mapping on the left part of the tensor.

With words, one could say that a splitting map of this form splits the space into the direct sum of
\imaginary(P_{i})
’s, and then sends to the left which
\imaginary(P_{i})
you are in, and to the right how you are mapped into a space
\mathcal{H}_{{\LL}_{I}}
that is the same for all
P_{i}
of a same equivalence class
I
. Let’s also add that the mapping to the right is coherent with what the algebra
\mathcal{A}
can do, i.e. an element of
\imaginary(P_{i})
and an element of
\imaginary(P_{k})
that are related by an operation of the considered algebra should have the same mapping in
\mathcal{H}_{{\LL}_{I}}
up to a scalar.

Let us first show that the choice of the maximal family of non-zero, orthogonal and pairwise orthogonal projectors for a fixed subalgebra
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
doesn’t matter. Indeed, we will prove in Lemma
B.1
that any two such families are equal up to unitary and in Lemma
B.2
that a canonical splitting map for
\mathcal{A}
can be written
\chi=\sum_{I}\sum_{i}(\outerproduct{i}{1_I}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{I}})UP_{1_{I}}AP_{i}
no matter the choice of such a family of projectors
\{P_{i}\}
.

Lemma B.1
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a subalgebra of
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
. Let
\{P_{i}\}
and
\{Q_{j}\}
be two maximal families of non-zero, orthogonal and pairwise orthogonal projectors of
\mathcal{A}
. Then there exists a unitary
U
in
\mathcal{A}
such that, up to reordering the projectors,
P_{i}=UQ_{i}U^{\dagger}
.

Proof.

Let a
\{P_{i}\}
and
\{Q_{j}\}
be two maximal families of non-zero, orthogonal and pairwise orthogonal projectors of
\mathcal{A}
. We have seen in (the proof of) Theorem
2.2
that one could construct a unitary
U
such that
U\mathcal{A}U^{\dagger}=\bigoplus_{I}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{I})\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{I}}
and that for a
P_{i}
in the equivalence class
I
,
UP_{i}U^{\dagger}=\outerproduct{i}{i}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{I}}
. Then, because the
Q_{j}
are elements of
\mathcal{A}
and
U
is unitary, we have that the
UQ_{j}U^{\dagger}=\sum_{I}Q_{j}^{I}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{I}}
are a maximal family of non-zero orthogonal and pairwise orthogonal projectors of
\bigoplus_{I}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{I})\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{I}}
. Because the sum is orthogonal, properties of the
Q_{j}
transfer to the
Q_{j}^{I}
which we can deduce are also orthogonal projectors. Moreover this also induces that for any two projectors
Q_{j_{1}}
and
Q_{j_{2}}
and any equivalence class
I
,
Q_{j_{1}}^{I}
and
Q_{j_{2}}^{I}
are orthogonal to each other. Non-zeroness tells us that for a fixed
j
, at least one of the
Q_{j}^{I}
is non zero, and maximality tells us that at most one is non-zero, otherwise we could replace
UQ_{j}U^{\dagger}
in the family by the different non-zero
Q_{j}^{I}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{I}}
appearing in its decomposition and which are orthogonal projectors, orthogonal to each other and also to all other
UQ_{k}U^{\dagger}
as we have seen that pairwise orthogonality is true in each block. Finally maximality also forces that
Q_{j}^{I}
is a rank 1 projector which we can then write
\outerproduct{\tilde{j}}{\tilde{j}}
. In particular this tells us that there is the same number of
P_{i}
and
Q_{j}
in each block and that they are all projectors of the same rank; and we can thus identify the
i
indices with the
j
ones. Let
V_{I}
be for each block
I
the unitary such that
V_{I}\ket{i}=\ket{\tilde{i}}
. Then
U^{\dagger}(\sum_{I}V_{I}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{I}})UP_{i}U^{\dagger}(\sum_{I}V_{I}^{\dagger}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{I}})U=Q_{i}
. Note that
(\sum_{I}V_{I}^{\dagger}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{I}})
is a unitary of
\bigoplus_{I}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{I})\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{I}}
and thus that
U^{\dagger}(\sum_{I}V_{I}^{\dagger}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{I}})U
is a unitary of
\mathcal{A}
.
∎

It follows that a canonical splitting map for
\mathcal{A}
can be decomposed with respect to any such family of non-zero, orthogonal and pairwise orthogonal projectors of
\mathcal{A}
.

Lemma B.2
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a subalgebra of
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
and
\chi
a canonical splitting map for
\mathcal{A}
. Let
\{Q_{j}\}
be any maximal family of non-zero, orthogonal and pairwise orthogonal projectors of
\mathcal{A}
. Then there exists a unitary
U
, an element
A
of
\mathcal{A}
and a basis
\{\ket{i}\}
of
\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{\chi}
such that

\chi=\sum_{I}\sum_{i}(\outerproduct{i}{1_I}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{I}})UQ_{1_{I}}AQ_{i}

(62)

Proof.

As seen previously,
\chi
can be written as
\chi=\sum_{I}\sum_{i}(\outerproduct{\tilde{i}}{1_I}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{I}})\tilde{U}P_{1_{I}}\tilde{B}P_{i}
with
\tilde{U}
a unitary,
\tilde{A}
an element of
\mathcal{A}
and
\{\ket{\tilde{i}}\}
a basis of
\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{\chi}
. By lemma
B.1
we know that there exists a unitary
V
in
\mathcal{A}
such that
P_{i}=VQ_{i}V^{\dagger}
. It follows that
\chi=\sum_{I}\sum_{i}(\outerproduct{\tilde{i}}{1_I}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{I}})\tilde{U}VQ_{1_{I}}V^{\dagger}\tilde{A}VQ_{i}V^{\dagger}
. By defining
U=\tilde{U}V
and
A=V^{\dagger}\tilde{A}V
(which is indeed such that for all
i\in I
,
Q_{1_{I}}AQ_{i}\neq 0
and in particular such that
Q_{1_{I}}AQ_{i}A^{\dagger}Q_{1_{I}}=Q_{1_{I}}
because
\tilde{A}
has this property with respect to the
P_{i}
), we get that
\chi=\sum_{I}\sum_{i}(\outerproduct{\tilde{i}}{1_I}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{I}})UQ_{1_{I}}AQ_{i}V^{\dagger}
. Moreover, as
V\in\mathcal{A}=\stloc(\chi)
, thus there exists
V_{\LL}\in\cons(\chi)
such that
(V_{\LL}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{\chi}})\chi=\chi V
(and
(V_{\LL}^{\dagger}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{\chi}})\chi=\chi V^{\dagger}
). It follows that
\chi=\chi V^{\dagger}V=(V_{\LL}^{\dagger}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{\chi}})\chi V
and by defining
\ket{i}=V_{\LL}\ket{\tilde{i}}
we get that
\chi=\sum_{I}\sum_{i}(\outerproduct{i}{1_I}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{I}})UQ_{1_{I}}AQ_{i}V^{\dagger}
which concludes the proof.
∎

Let us now prove Proposition
4.4
. We have that
\zeta=\sum_{J}\sum_{j}(\outerproduct{j}{1_J}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{J}})VQ_{1_{J}}BQ_{j}
with the
\{Q_{j}\}
a maximal family of non-zero, orthogonal and pairwise orthogonal projectors of
\mathcal{A}_{S}
. Let us define the right family of such projectors of
\mathcal{A}_{B}
that will make our computation possible. If the family
\{Q_{j}\}
is also maximal for
\mathcal{A}_{B}
(which doesn’t necessarily mean that
\mathcal{A}_{B}=\mathcal{A}_{S}
, as we can have
Q_{j}\mathcal{A}_{B}Q_{k}\neq Q_{j}\mathcal{A}_{S}Q_{k}
), we define
\{P_{i}\}=\{Q_{j}\}
. Otherwise, if
\{Q_{j}\}
is not maximal for
\mathcal{A}_{B}
, there exists a maximal family
\{P_{i}\}
of such projectors for
\mathcal{A}_{B}
such that the
P_{i}
generates the
Q_{j}
. Because the
P_{i}
are orthogonal, pairwise orthogonal and because the
Q_{j}
also form a family of projectors of
\mathcal{A}_{B}
with the same properties, just not a maximal one, it follows that each of the
Q_{j}
can be written as a sum of some of the
P_{i}
, all with coefficient
1
; such that each
P_{i}
appears in the decomposition of precisely one
Q_{j}
. This means that we can reindex the
P_{i}
as
P_{i}^{j}
(for a fixed
j
, the
i
index varies from
1
to a number
n_{j}
and indicates which projector of the decomposition we are considering). However this
\{P_{i}^{j}\}
family might not have all the properties we need thus we will build a new one
\{\tilde{P}_{i}^{j}\}
.

Let us work in one equivalence class
J
of the
Q_{j}
(as projectors of
\mathcal{A}_{S}
). Consider
Q_{1}=\sum_{i}P_{i}^{1}
, a projector of the equivalence class; we define
\{\tilde{P}_{i}^{1}\}=\{P_{i}^{1}\}
. Let us now fix a
j\in J
; by definition of the equivalence class there exists
A\in\mathcal{A}_{S}
such that
Q_{1}AQ_{j}\neq 0
. If we multiply
A
by the right scalar we can even have that
Q_{j}A^{\dagger}Q_{1}AQ_{j}=Q_{j}
which means that we can use the decomposition of
Q_{1}
to get a new decomposition of
Q_{j}
as
Q_{j}=\sum_{i}Q_{j}A^{\dagger}P_{i}^{1}AQ_{j}
. And we define
\tilde{P}_{i}^{j}=Q_{j}A^{\dagger}P_{i}^{1}AQ_{j}
. Doing that for each
j\in J
and then for each equivalence class
J
defines a new family
\{\tilde{P}_{i}^{j}\}
of elements of
\mathcal{A}_{B}
. Morally we are making sure that each
Q_{j}
is decomposed in the ”same way” and that each element of its decomposition
\tilde{P}_{i}^{j}
precisely corresponds (for what the algebra
\mathcal{A}_{B}
can do) to an element
\tilde{P}_{i}^{k}
of the decomposition of another
Q_{k}
. Also note that the
A\in\mathcal{A}_{S}
that we choose is a priori different for each pair
Q_{j},Q_{k}
but one can prove that there exists a, this time unique,
A
that does the job for all pairs
Q_{j},Q_{k}
. We choose this
A
for the rest of the proof. It remains to show that
\{\tilde{P}_{i}^{j}\}
is a maximal family of non-zero, orthogonal and pairwise orthogonal projectors of
\mathcal{A}_{B}
.

First we remark that for all
i_{1}\neq i_{2}
and for all
B\in\mathcal{A}_{S}
,

\begin{split}\tilde{P}_{i_{1}}^{1}B\tilde{P}_{i_{2}}^{1}&=\tilde{P}_{i_{1}}^{1}Q_{1}BQ_{1}\tilde{P}_{i_{2}}^{1}\\
&=\alpha\tilde{P}_{i_{1}}^{1}Q_{1}\tilde{P}_{i_{2}}^{1}\\
&=\alpha\tilde{P}_{i_{1}}^{1}\tilde{P}_{i_{2}}^{1}\\
&=0\end{split}

(63)

It follows that the
\tilde{P}_{i}^{j}
are pairwise orthogonal projectors. Indeed if
j\neq k

\tilde{P}_{i_{1}}^{j}\tilde{P}_{i_{2}}^{k}=\tilde{P}_{i_{1}}^{j}Q_{j}Q_{k}\tilde{P}_{i_{2}}^{k}=0

(64)

by orthogonality of
Q_{j}
and
Q_{k}
. Now suppose that
j=k
,

\tilde{P}_{i_{1}}^{j}\tilde{P}_{i_{2}}^{j}=Q_{j}A^{\dagger}P_{i_{1}}^{1}AQ_{j}A^{\dagger}P_{i_{2}}^{1}AQ_{j}

(65)

If
i_{1}\neq i_{2}
, by
63
we have that
P_{i_{1}}^{1}AQ_{j}A^{\dagger}P_{i_{2}}^{1}=0
. However in the case where
i_{1}=i_{2}
, because the
P_{i}^{1}
are elements of
\{P_{i}\}
they are such that for all
B\in\mathcal{A}_{B}
there exists a
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
such that
P_{i}^{1}BP_{i}^{1}=\lambda P_{i}^{1}
; and we get that

\begin{split}\tilde{P}_{i_{1}}^{j}\tilde{P}_{i_{2}}^{j}&=Q_{j}A^{\dagger}P_{i_{1}}^{1}AQ_{j}A^{\dagger}P_{i_{2}}^{1}AQ_{j}\\
&=\lambda Q_{j}A^{\dagger}P_{i_{1}}^{1}AQ_{j}\\
&=\lambda\tilde{P}_{i_{1}}^{j}\end{split}

(66)

Here,
\lambda=1
as

\begin{split}P_{i_{1}}^{1}AQ_{j}A^{\dagger}P_{i_{1}}^{1}&=P_{i_{1}}^{1}Q_{1}AQ_{j}A^{\dagger}Q_{1}P_{i_{1}}^{1}\\
&=\tilde{P}_{i_{1}}^{1}Q_{1}\tilde{P}_{i_{1}}^{1}\\
&=\tilde{P}_{i_{1}}^{1}\end{split}

(67)

by the choice of
A
. This proves that the
\tilde{P}_{i}^{j}
form a family of pairwise orthogonal projectors. The
\tilde{P}_{i}^{j}
are moreover orthogonal by construction. It remains to prove that this family is maximal. Suppose that this new family is not maximal, it implies that one of the
\tilde{P}_{i}^{j}
could be decomposed as
\tilde{P}_{i}^{j}=R_{1}+R_{2}
with these two new projectors being orthogonal, orthogonal to each other as well as to any
\tilde{P}_{i^{\prime}}^{j^{\prime}}
. This would give a more fine-grained decomposition of
Q_{j}
and by applying the above reasoning in the reverse direction (transferring this time the decomposition of
Q_{j}
to
Q_{1}
) we could refine the decomposition of
Q_{1}
which is already a decomposition given by a maximal family and cannot be fine-grained! Note that with this decomposition of the
Q_{j}
as a sum of
\tilde{P}_{i}^{j}
, if
Q_{j}
and
Q_{k}
are projectors of the same equivalence class
J
, every
\tilde{P}_{i}^{j}
has a unique corresponding projector in the decomposition of
Q_{k}
:
\tilde{P}_{i}^{k}
, such that
\tilde{P}_{i}^{j}\mathcal{A}_{S}\tilde{P}_{i}^{k}\neq 0
. Every
Q_{j}
has the same number of terms in his
\tilde{P}_{i}^{j}
decomposition and the
\{\tilde{P}_{i}^{j},\tilde{P}_{i}^{k},\tilde{P}_{i}^{l},...\}
form equivalence classes that can be represented by the index
i
.

We just proved that
\{\tilde{P}_{i}^{j}\}
was a maximal family of non-zero, orthogonal and pairwise orthogonal projectors and thus, by Lemma
B.2
,
\chi
can be written as

\chi=\sum_{J}\sum_{j\in J}\sum_{i=1,...,n_{j}}(\outerproduct{i,j}{u_{1_{ij}},d_{1_{ij}}}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{I}})U\tilde{P}_{d_{1_{ij}}}^{u_{1_{ij}}}B\tilde{P}_{i}^{j}

(68)

where
1_{ij}
is the pair of up and down indices of
\tilde{P}_{d_{1_{ij}}}^{u_{1_{ij}}}
, a chosen representative of the class of
\tilde{P}_{i}^{j}
for the block equivalence relation of
\{\tilde{P}_{i}^{j}\}
as elements of
\mathcal{A}_{B}
, i.e.
\tilde{P}_{i}^{j}R_{B}\tilde{P}_{k}^{l}\Leftrightarrow\tilde{P}_{i}^{j}\mathcal{A}_{B}\tilde{P}_{k}^{l}\neq 0
. It shouldn’t be confused with
1_{J}
the index of the chosen representative of all
Q_{j}
in the
J
-class of the equivalence relation
Q_{j}R_{S}Q_{k}\Leftrightarrow Q_{j}\mathcal{A}_{S}Q_{j}\neq 0
. Which are both different from the equivalence relation
\tilde{P}_{i}^{j}R\tilde{P}_{k}^{l}\Leftrightarrow\tilde{P}_{i}^{j}\mathcal{A}_{S}\tilde{P}_{k}^{l}\neq 0
where, as we have seen,
\tilde{P}_{i}^{j}
and
\tilde{P}_{k}^{l}
are in the same class if and only if
j
and
l
are in the same class for
R_{S}
and
i=k
.

We remind that
\zeta
is of the form

\zeta=\sum_{J}\sum_{j\in J}(\outerproduct{j}{1_J}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{J}})VQ_{1_{J}}AQ_{j}

(69)

It remains to show that
\zeta\sqsubseteq\chi
which we will do be constructing the isometries
\bigcirc
and
.

The white dot acts on the left output of
\chi
, so its input is a
\ket{i,j}
telling us in which
\tilde{P}_{i}^{j}
we are. From this information it will send the
j
on the left, telling in which
Q_{j}
we are (and this information matches the left side of
\zeta
). And it sends the
i
on the right, telling in which class of
\tilde{P}_{i}^{j}
(for
\mathcal{A}_{S}
) we are.

Now the black dot. It acts on the right output of
\zeta
so it acts on how elements of
\oplus_{j}\imaginary(Q_{j})
have been mapped into
\oplus_{J}\mathcal{H}_{{\LL}_{J}}
. However it doesn’t have in input the information that goes on the left of
\zeta
, i.e. from which
\imaginary(Q_{j})
it comes precisely. The difficulty is then to show that this information doesn’t matter to recover how the element of
\mathcal{H}
would have been mapped on the right through
\chi
. The idea to construct the black dot is the following: we don’t know the left part of
\ket{y}
so we might as well map it to
\ket{1_J}\otimes\ket{y}
. This is isometric. Then we undo
\zeta
and do
\chi
which is isometric because applied on
\imaginary(\zeta)
. Finally as we didn’t know in which
Q_{j}
we were in the first place and we have made up the left part
\ket{1_J}
, the left part
\ket{i,j}
that we get in the end is false a priori. That’s why instead we’re only going to keep the information of the equivalence class of
\tilde{P}_{i}^{j}
, i.e.
i
. By doing so we match the type of the right output of the white dot and and keep the whole process isometric.

It remains to show that we recover on the right the mapping that
\chi
would have done and that the equivalence class on the left of the black dot is the same as the one on the right of the white dot.

Let
\ket{x}\in\imaginary(\tilde{P}_{k}^{p})\subseteq\mathcal{H}
. It is mapped by
\chi
to
\ket{k,p}\otimes\ket{y}
and mapped by
\zeta
to
\ket{p}\otimes\ket{z}
.

(\bigcirc\otimes\mathbb{1})\chi\ket{x}=\ket{p}\otimes\ket{k}\otimes\ket{y}.

(70)

(\mathbb{1}\otimes\hbox to8.4pt{\vbox to8.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.2pt\lower-4.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 5.53 0 C 5.53 3.06 3.06 5.53 0 5.53 C -3.06 5.53 -5.53 3.06 -5.53 0 C -5.53 -3.06 -3.06 -5.53 0 -5.53 C 3.06 -5.53 5.53 -3.06 5.53 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}})\zeta\ket{x}=\ket{p}\otimes((\ket{i,j}\rightarrow\ket{i})\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{B}})W_{B}W_{S}^{\dagger}(\ket{1_{P}}\otimes\ket{z})

(71)

First let’s remark that

\begin{split}\zeta(Q_{1_{P}}AQ_{p})\ket{x}&=[\sum_{J}\sum_{j\in J}(\outerproduct{j}{1_J}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{J}})VQ_{1_{J}}AQ_{j}](Q_{1_{P}}AQ_{p})\ket{x}\\
&=(\outerproduct{1_{P}}{1_{P}}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{{P}}})V(Q_{1_{P}}AQ_{p})\ket{x}\\
&=(\outerproduct{1_{P}}{p}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{{P}}})W_{S}\ket{x}\\
&=(\outerproduct{1_{P}}{p}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{{P}}})\ket{p}\otimes\ket{z}\\
&=\ket{1_{P}}\otimes\ket{z}\end{split}

(72)

which means that

\begin{split}&\chi\zeta^{\dagger}(\ket{1_{P}}\otimes\ket{z})\\
=&\chi\zeta^{\dagger}\zeta(Q_{1_{P}}AQ_{p})\ket{x}\\
=&[\sum_{J}\sum_{j\in J}\sum_{i=1,...,n_{j}}(\outerproduct{i,j}{u_{1_{ij}},d_{1_{ij}}}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{J}})U\tilde{P}_{d_{1_{ij}}}^{u_{1_{ij}}}B\tilde{P}_{i}^{j}](Q_{1_{P}}AQ_{p})\ket{x}\\
=&\sum_{i=1,...,n_{1_{P}}}(\outerproduct{i,1_{{P}}}{u_{1_{i1_{{P}}}},d_{1_{i1_{{P}}}}}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{{P}}})U\tilde{P}_{d_{1_{i1_{{P}}}}}^{u_{1_{i1_{{P}}}}}B\tilde{P}_{i}^{1_{{P}}})(Q_{1_{P}}AQ_{p})\ket{x}\\
=&\sum_{i=1,...,n_{1_{P}}}(\outerproduct{i,1_{{P}}}{u_{1_{i1_{{P}}}},d_{1_{i1_{{P}}}}}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{{P}}})U\tilde{P}_{d_{1_{i1_{{P}}}}}^{u_{1_{i1_{{P}}}}}B\tilde{P}_{i}^{1_{{P}}}A\tilde{P}_{i}^{p}\ket{x}\\
=&(\outerproduct{k,1_{{P}}}{u_{1_{k1_{{P}}}},d_{1_{k1_{{P}}}}}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{{P}}})U\tilde{P}_{d_{1_{k1_{{P}}}}}^{u_{1_{k1_{{P}}}}}B\tilde{P}_{k}^{1_{{P}}}A\tilde{P}_{k}^{p}\ket{x}\\
=&(\outerproduct{k,1_{P}}{k,p}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{{P}}})(\outerproduct{k,p}{u_{1_{k1_{{P}}}},d_{1_{k1_{{P}}}}}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{{P}}})U\tilde{P}_{d_{1_{k1_{{P}}}}}^{u_{1_{k1_{{P}}}}}B\tilde{P}_{k}^{p}\ket{x}\\
=&(\outerproduct{k,1_{P}}{k,p}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{{P}}})W_{B}\ket{x}\\
=&(\outerproduct{k,1_{P}}{k,p}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{{P}}})\ket{k,p}\otimes\ket{y}\\
=&\ket{k,1_{P}}\otimes\ket{y}\end{split}

(73)

It follows that

\begin{split}&((\ket{i,j}\rightarrow\ket{i})\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{B}})W_{B}W_{S}^{\dagger}(\ket{1_{P}}\otimes\ket{z})\\
=&((\ket{i,j}\rightarrow\ket{i})\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{B}})\ket{k,1_{P}}\otimes\ket{y}\\
=&\ket{k}\otimes\ket{y}\end{split}

(74)

and finally that

(\mathbb{1}\otimes\hbox to6.4pt{\vbox to6.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.2pt\lower-3.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 4.15 0 C 4.15 2.29 2.29 4.15 0 4.15 C -2.29 4.15 -4.15 2.29 -4.15 0 C -4.15 -2.29 -2.29 -4.15 0 -4.15 C 2.29 -4.15 4.15 -2.29 4.15 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}})\zeta\ket{x}=\ket{p}\otimes\ket{k}\otimes\ket{y}=(\bigcirc\otimes\mathbb{1})\chi\ket{x}

(75)

which concludes the proof.
∎

Appendix C
Correspondence between diagrammatic and algebraic semi-causality definitions

Let us prove, in this last appendix, that for each of the Heisenberg and the Schrödinger semi-causality, the algebraic definition and the diagrammatic one are actually equivalent. We begin by the Heisenberg semi-causality of which we remind the definition.

Definition C.1

(Heisenberg semi-causality)
.

We say that a unitary
U:\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}
is Heisenberg semi-causal from
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
to
\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{K})
if

[U^{\dagger}\mathcal{B}U,\mathcal{A}]=0.

In terms of splitting maps, this is equivalent to asking that for any choice of lean splitting maps
\chi_{\mathcal{A}}
,
\chi_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}
and
\chi_{\mathcal{B}}
representing the corresponding algebras and for any
B\in\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{\chi_{\mathcal{B}}}
, there exists
A_{1}\in\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{\chi_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}}
and
A_{2}\in\mathcal{H}_{\R}^{\chi_{\mathcal{A}}}
such that

U^{\dagger}
B
U
\chi_{\mathcal{B}}
\chi_{\mathcal{B}}
=
A_{1}
\chi_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}
\chi_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}
=
\chi_{\mathcal{A}}
\chi_{\mathcal{A}}
A_{2}

(76)

Proof.

Suppose that
[U^{\dagger}\mathcal{B}U,\mathcal{A}]=0
. Let
\chi_{\mathcal{A}}
,
\chi_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}
and
\chi_{\mathcal{B}}
be lean splitting maps representing the corresponding algebras and let
B\in\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{\chi_{\mathcal{B}}}
. Because all the splitting maps are lean and thus balanced, we know that
\loc(\chi_{\mathcal{B}})=\stloc(\chi_{\mathcal{B}})=\mathcal{B}
and similarly for
\chi_{\mathcal{A}}
and
\chi_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}
. It follows that
\chi_{\mathcal{B}}^{\dagger}(B\otimes\mathbb{1})\chi_{\mathcal{B}}\in\mathcal{B}
and thus that
U^{\dagger}\chi_{\mathcal{B}}^{\dagger}(B\otimes\mathbb{1})\chi_{\mathcal{B}}U\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}=\loc_{\LL}(\chi_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}})=\loc_{\R}(\chi_{\mathcal{A}})
which proves the existence of
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
. The reverse implication is proved by the same arguments.
∎

Let us now focus on the Schrödinger semi-causality of which we remind the definitions.

Definition C.2

(Schrödinger semi-causality)
.

We say that a channel
\mathcal{E}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})\rightarrow\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{K})
is Schrödinger semi-causal from
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
to
\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{K})
if there exists a completely positive and trace-preserving (CPTP) map
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
such that

\Tr_{\mathcal{B^{\prime}}}[\mathcal{E}(\cdot)]=\mathcal{E}^{\prime}(\Tr_{\mathcal{A}}[\cdot]).

This condition can equivalently be phrased as the fact that for any choice of lean splitting maps
\chi_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}
and
\chi_{\mathcal{B}}
there exists a CPTP map
\tilde{\mathcal{E}}
such that

\chi_{\mathcal{B}}
\mathcal{E}
\chi_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}
\tilde{\mathcal{E}}
=

(77)

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{E}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})\rightarrow\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{K})
be a channel and suppose that there exists a CPTP map
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
such that
\Tr_{\mathcal{B^{\prime}}}[\mathcal{E}(\cdot)]=\mathcal{E}^{\prime}(\Tr_{\mathcal{A}}[\cdot])
. Let
\chi_{\mathcal{B}}
and
\chi_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}
be lean splitting maps for
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
respectively. By Theorem
5.1
, we know that there exists isometries
U
and
V
such that
\Tr_{\chi_{\mathcal{B}}}(\cdot)=U\Tr_{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}(\cdot)U^{\dagger}
and
\Tr_{\chi_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}}(\cdot)=V\Tr_{\mathcal{A}}(\cdot)V^{\dagger}
. It follows that
\Tr_{\chi_{\mathcal{B}}}(\mathcal{E}(\cdot))=U\Tr_{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{E}(\cdot))U^{\dagger}=U\mathcal{E}^{\prime}(\Tr_{\mathcal{A}}(\cdot))U^{\dagger}=U\mathcal{E}^{\prime}(V^{\dagger}\Tr_{\chi_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}}(\cdot)V)U^{\dagger}
. The result doesn’t immediately follow as the action of
V^{\dagger}
is not trace-preserving. However remark that
VV^{\dagger}\Tr_{\chi_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}}(\cdot)VV^{\dagger}
. We can thus extend
V^{\dagger}
to a
W
such that
W\ket{x}=V\ket{x}
if
\ket{x}\in\imaginary(V)
and
W\ket{x}=|\ket{x}|\ket{y}
where
\ket{y}\in\mathcal{H}_{\LL}^{\chi_{\mathcal{B}}}
is of norm one. The action of
W
is trace-preserving. And
W\Tr_{\chi_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}}(\cdot)W^{\dagger}=WVV^{\dagger}\Tr_{\chi_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}}(\cdot)VV^{\dagger}W^{\dagger}=V^{\dagger}VV^{\dagger}\Tr_{\chi_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}}(\cdot)VV^{\dagger}V=V^{\dagger}\Tr_{\chi_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}}(\cdot)V
. Defining
\tilde{\mathcal{E}}(\cdot)=U\mathcal{E}^{\prime}(W\cdot W^{\dagger})U^{\dagger}
which is now a CPTP map makes
\mathcal{E}
diagrammatically semi-causal. A similar construction proves the reverse implication.
∎

Appendix D
Relation between two Stinespring dilations of a map

Lemma D.1
.

Let
\mathcal{E}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})\rightarrow\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{K})
be a quantum channel. Let
U:\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}\otimes\mathcal{K}_{U}
and
V:\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}\otimes\mathcal{K}_{V}
be two dilations of
\mathcal{E}
, i.e. isometries such that
\mathcal{E}(\cdot)=\Tr_{\mathcal{K}_{U}}(U\cdot U^{\dagger})=\Tr_{\mathcal{K}_{V}}(V\cdot V^{\dagger})
, and suppose that
\dim(\mathcal{K}_{U})\leq\dim(\mathcal{K}_{V})
. Then there exists an isometry
W:\mathcal{K}_{U}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}_{V}
such that
V=(\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{K}}\otimes W)U
.

Proof.

A minimal dilation of
\mathcal{E}
, is a dilation
T:\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}\otimes\mathcal{K}_{T}
such that the set
\{(A\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{K}_{T}})T\ket{\phi},\ket{\phi}\in\mathcal{H},A\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{K})\}
spans
\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathcal{K}_{T}
. Consider such a dilation. By minimality of
T
, there exists
[
24
]
isometries
W_{U}:\mathcal{K}_{T}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}_{U}
and
W_{V}:\mathcal{K}_{T}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}_{V}
such that
U=(\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}}\otimes W_{U})T
and
V=(\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}}\otimes W_{V})T
. Remark that, being an isometry,
W_{U}
is a unitary into its image. It follows that
W_{V}W_{U}^{\dagger}
acts isometrically from
\imaginary(W_{U})
(to
\imaginary(W_{V})
) and is zero on
\imaginary(W_{U})^{\perp}
. Then because
rank(W_{U})=rank(W_{V})=\dim(\mathcal{K}_{T})\leq\dim(\mathcal{K}_{U})\leq\dim(\mathcal{K}_{V})
, one can construct an isometry
S:\imaginary(W_{U})^{\perp}\rightarrow\imaginary(W_{V})^{\perp}\subseteq\mathcal{K}_{V}
. And we can define the isometry
W=(W_{V}W_{U}^{\dagger})_{|\imaginary(W_{U})}\oplus S:\mathcal{K}_{U}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}_{V}
, such that
(\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{K}}\otimes W)U=(\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{K}}\otimes W)(\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{K}}\otimes W_{U})T=(\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{K}}\otimes W_{V}W_{U}^{\dagger}W_{U})T=(\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{K}}\otimes W_{V})T=V
.
∎
